# Bongbong Marcos, Philippines, 2023

## 1. Overview

Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr. held power throughout 2023 as president of the Philippines, commander-in-chief, head of the executive branch, and concurrent secretary of agriculture for much of the period. His authority was substantial but constrained by Congress, courts, constitutional commissions, local political dynasties, the military and police bureaucracy, coalition partners—including Vice President Sara Duterte—and dependence on foreign trade, investment, and security relationships.

The period combined an outwardly active foreign-policy reset toward the United States and Japan with continuing domestic rights problems inherited from Rodrigo Duterte. The administration pursued infrastructure, investment, agricultural, and food-price objectives. Economic growth remained positive, but inflation and rice prices generated political pressure. Reliable information was available from courts, oversight agencies, international organizations, and independent media, but human-rights reporting was impaired by intimidation, red-tagging, disappearances, official denial, and weak accountability.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record BM-2023-01 — Marcos held executive power while operating within a competitive constitutional system

**Claim and significance.** Marcos was the sitting Philippine president throughout 2023. He exercised national executive authority and command over the security apparatus, but policy implementation depended on Congress, constitutional bodies, courts, local governments, and coalition politics. This matters because national indicators cannot automatically be attributed to him personally; attribution is strongest where he issued orders, signed laws, or publicly directed policy.

**Strongest source.** The official Philippine executive orders and government releases identify Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. as president and show him approving national policy measures, including the rice price ceiling and Maharlika Investment Fund.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** High for presidential decisions and executive orders; moderate for broad outcomes involving agencies or Congress; low for inherited structural conditions.

**Cautions and complications.** Government sources describe policy intent and official accomplishments, not independent effectiveness. Presidential control over local government, police conduct, and military operations is real but mediated through bureaucratic and political networks.

**Independent support.** Human Rights Watch, Freedom House, the Commission on Human Rights, and BTI all treat Marcos as the responsible national executive while distinguishing his administration from inherited Duterte-era abuses. [Human Rights Watch, “Philippines: Marcos Failing on Rights”](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/06/28/philippines-marcos-failing-rights); [BTI 2024 Philippines Country Report](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_PHL.pdf).

### Record BM-2023-02 — The administration reoriented security policy toward the United States and Japan

**Claim and significance.** In 2023 Marcos deepened defense and diplomatic cooperation with the United States and Japan, including implementation and expansion of Enhanced Defense Cooperation Agreement arrangements and stronger public opposition to Chinese coercion in the South China Sea. This reduced strategic ambiguity but increased exposure to great-power confrontation and domestic criticism over sovereignty and alliance dependence.

**Strongest source.** The Asia Maritime Transparency Initiative’s analyses document the strategic shift, the EDCA expansion, and domestic resistance. [“Marcos Jr. Steers Manila toward Washington and Tokyo”](https://amti.csis.org/marcos-jr-steers-manila-toward-washington-and-tokyo); [“More Than Meets the Eye: Philippine Upgrades at EDCA Sites”](https://amti.csis.org/more-than-meets-the-eye-philippine-upgrades-at-edca-sites).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023, with some analysis published afterward.

**Ruler connection.** High: Marcos personally directed the foreign-policy change and publicly framed the Philippines’ position.

**Complicating evidence.** The policy was not simply pro-American alignment. Analysts describe it as flexible balancing, with Manila retaining economic and diplomatic engagement with China. China-related confrontations also reflected long-running territorial disputes and alliance commitments predating Marcos.

**Cautions.** AMTI is a specialist policy-analysis institution associated with a US think tank; it is useful for strategic interpretation but should be paired with Philippine, Chinese, ASEAN, and official diplomatic records.

### Record BM-2023-03 — South China Sea confrontations increased the administration’s security burden

**Claim and significance.** Philippine and Chinese vessels repeatedly confronted one another in disputed South China Sea waters during 2023, including incidents involving blocking, water-cannoning, shadowing, and collisions. Marcos’s government publicized Chinese actions and sought diplomatic and allied support. These events created a significant military-escalation risk without producing an armed conflict.

**Strongest source.** Associated Press reporting describes the confrontations and the Philippine government’s decision to publicize Chinese conduct. [Chinese and Philippine ship collision](https://apnews.com/article/06e9fe0ef440aba09bc650d986d83377); [Philippines launches strategy of publicizing Chinese actions](https://apnews.com/article/724c054c4f3b1c363a257334dd13); [Marcos: China Laser Not Enough to Activate US Defense Pact](https://apnews.com/article/878a88b8663bf52e2e93591228312554).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** High for diplomatic response and public communication; moderate for the underlying confrontations, which involved Chinese and Philippine state agencies operating within a longstanding dispute.

**Complicating evidence.** Marcos avoided treating every confrontation as an armed attack triggering the US mutual-defense treaty. The administration therefore combined deterrence and alliance signaling with escalation management.

**Cautions.** News reports rely partly on statements from the governments involved, each of which has an interest in presenting the other as responsible. Independent maritime evidence and official logs should be checked for individual incidents.

### Record BM-2023-04 — Rights abuses and impunity continued under Marcos

**Claim and significance.** Human Rights Watch reported that police and state agents continued drug-related killings, arbitrary arrests, enforced disappearances, and harassment of activists under Marcos, although at a lower rate than during Duterte’s presidency. It also reported continued red-tagging and unresolved prosecutions involving Leila de Lima and Maria Ressa.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch’s June 2023 assessment states that the administration had made little measurable progress on human-rights protection, citing 336 drug-related killings reported since Marcos took office and only three police convictions across thousands of drug-war cases since 2016. [Human Rights Watch, “Philippines: Marcos Failing on Rights,” June 28, 2023](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/06/28/philippines-marcos-failing-rights).

**Period status.** Directly concerns the first year of Marcos’s presidency and includes inherited Duterte-era patterns.

**Ruler connection.** High for the administration’s failure to rescind operative drug-war policies, pursue accountability, or stop official red-tagging; lower for killings committed by individual officers without evidence of presidential direction.

**Complicating evidence.** The government claimed progress and invited UN engagement. A later UN special-rapporteur visit recognized some governmental steps while still calling for stronger accountability. [OHCHR, “Report on the Situation of Human Rights in the Philippines”](https://bangkok.ohchr.org/reports/ohchr-report-situation-human-rights-philippines).

**Cautions.** Human Rights Watch is an independent advocacy organization and is explicit about its normative conclusions. Its quantitative claims should be reconciled with Philippine police, CHR, and court records.

### Record BM-2023-05 — The Commission on Human Rights documented continuing serious violations

**Claim and significance.** The Philippine Commission on Human Rights recorded 93 alleged extrajudicial-killing cases, 35 torture cases, and 12 enforced-disappearance cases in 2023. It also reported that the right to life constituted the largest category of civil-rights violations in its caseload. These figures show that serious abuses remained a live governance problem even if they do not establish that Marcos personally ordered them.

**Strongest source.** [Commission on Human Rights of the Philippines, 2023 Annual Accomplishment Report, pp. 5–8](https://cdn1.chr.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/06100614/CHR-2023-Annual-Accomplishment-Report.pdf).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Moderate. The CHR is a constitutional commission operating during Marcos’s presidency, but its figures are case reports and complaints, not judicial findings attributing responsibility to the president.

**Complicating evidence.** The same report documents substantial investigative, legal-assistance, monitoring, and victim-support work by the CHR, including 3,055 disposed cases out of 6,544 handled and assistance to hundreds of affected individuals.

**Cautions.** The report is an institutional accomplishment report and may emphasize the commission’s own activities. Reported cases are not equivalent to proven violations, and underreporting is likely where victims fear retaliation.

### Record BM-2023-06 — Marcos used executive intervention to address rice-price inflation

**Claim and significance.** On August 31, 2023, Marcos approved Executive Order No. 39, imposing nationwide price ceilings of ₱41 per kilogram for regular-milled rice and ₱45 for well-milled rice. The measure responded to rising rice prices and alleged hoarding or market manipulation. The government lifted the ceilings in October after reporting price stabilization.

**Strongest source.** [Philippine Presidential Communications Office, “PBBM sets price ceilings on rice nationwide,” August 31, 2023](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-sets-price-ceilings-on-rice-nationwide/); [EO No. 42, lifting the ceilings](https://lawphil.net/executive/execord/eo2023/pdf/eo_42_2023.pdf).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** High: the order was issued on Marcos’s approval and he was concurrently agriculture secretary.

**Complicating evidence.** The official explanation emphasized consumer protection and supply problems, while policy commentary warned that price controls could harm retailers and farmers or create shortages. The government’s claim that the cap stabilized prices should be checked against Philippine Statistics Authority price series and market-level evidence.

**Cautions.** Official releases are strong evidence that the policy occurred, but weak evidence for its causal success. The price measure was temporary and operated amid international rice-market disruption, import restrictions, and domestic supply conditions.

### Record BM-2023-07 — The Maharlika Investment Fund was established amid governance concerns

**Claim and significance.** Marcos signed Republic Act No. 11954 on July 18, 2023, creating the Philippines’ first sovereign wealth fund. The administration presented it as a vehicle for infrastructure, investment, and economic growth. Because the fund mobilized public financial assets and created new governance arrangements, it became a significant test of fiscal prudence, transparency, and conflict-of-interest controls.

**Strongest source.** [Philippine Department of Finance, “Newly enacted Maharlika Investment Fund to serve as vehicle for growth,” July 18, 2023](https://www.dof.gov.ph/newly-enacted-maharlika-investment-fund-to-serve-as-vehicle-for-growth/).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** High for signing and executive implementation; shared with Congress, which passed the enabling legislation.

**Complicating evidence.** The administration described the measure as an investment and development instrument, while media-monitoring and policy sources raised concerns about weak justification, governance safeguards, and the use of public funds. [CMFR, “Maharlika fund proposal needs more media scrutiny”](https://cmfr-phil.org/media-ethics-responsibility/journalism-review/cmfr-media-review/maharlika-fund-proposal-needs-more-media-scrutiny).

**Cautions.** Early 2023 evidence mostly concerns design and political debate, not realized investment returns. Later audits and fund performance are needed before judging effectiveness or integrity.

### Record BM-2023-08 — Economic performance was positive but living-cost pressure remained

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank estimated Philippine GDP growth at approximately 5.6 percent in 2023 and emphasized that high inflation, elevated interest rates, weak trade, and global uncertainty constrained the recovery. Poverty fell to 15.5 percent in 2023 according to the World Bank’s country profile, but this did not eliminate substantial hardship or food-price pressure.

**Strongest source.** [World Bank, “Implementing Reforms to Boost Private Investment,” December 5, 2023](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph); [World Bank Philippines country profile](https://www.worldbank.org/ext/en/country/philippines).

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Moderate. Macroeconomic outcomes were jointly produced by the central bank, Congress, global conditions, remittances, private demand, and inherited policy settings.

**Complicating evidence.** Growth and poverty reduction support an effectiveness claim, while inflation and low-productivity employment complicate it. Current-dollar national-account changes must not be interpreted as real welfare growth without price adjustment.

**Cautions.** World Bank estimates and poverty measures are authoritative but revised over time and depend on survey timing, price assumptions, and methodological thresholds.

## 3. Information environment and limitations

Information was comparatively accessible for executive orders, budgets, macroeconomic indicators, legislation, and diplomatic statements. The CHR, World Bank, BTI, OHCHR, and independent media provide useful checks on official claims.

The most difficult subjects were killings, disappearances, red-tagging, police abuses, and military counterinsurgency. Victims and witnesses faced intimidation; activists and journalists were publicly labeled as communist supporters; official agencies sometimes disputed disappearances or presented contested “surrenders”; and police statistics may undercount deaths. Political polarization and the Marcos family’s contested historical legacy also produced competing narratives and misinformation.

The evidence supports strong attribution for signed laws, executive orders, diplomatic choices, and public policy priorities. It supports weaker attribution for aggregate growth, poverty, crime, and human-rights outcomes unless agency records, courts, or credible investigations connect events to presidential decisions or institutional practices.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

- What concrete directives did Marcos issue regarding drug-war killings, red-tagging, disappearances, and NTF-ELCAC operations?
- How many 2023 cases involving police or military personnel produced indictments, convictions, or disciplinary action?
- Did the rice price ceiling improve consumer prices without reducing farmer or retailer income?
- What were the Maharlika Fund’s funding sources, conflict-of-interest safeguards, board appointments, and audit findings?
- How did Congress, local dynasties, Sara Duterte, and the military influence presidential appointments and policy?
- What measurable progress occurred under the Philippine Development Plan 2023–2028?
- Did expanded US/Japan defense cooperation reduce coercion or increase escalation risk in the South China Sea?
- What independent evidence exists on education quality, health access, food insecurity, and household living standards in 2023?

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Department of Foreign Affairs FY 2023 Accomplishment Report.
- Commission on Audit annual reports concerning Maharlika, NTF-ELCAC, and security spending.
- Philippine Statistics Authority 2023 inflation, rice-price, poverty, labor, education, and health tables.
- Supreme Court and trial-court records involving Leila de Lima, Maria Ressa, activists, and alleged state abuses.
- Official NTF-ELCAC, AFP, and CHR records concerning Jonila Castro and Jhed Tamano.
- Philippine Development Report 2023 implementation annexes.
- Detailed source examination of the Asia Foundation’s 2023 country report and the Ateneo misinformation study.
- Independent Philippine investigative reporting on political appointments, dynastic ties, and Maharlika governance.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Chapter 1B — Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility

Ruler: Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr.  
Country: Philippines  
Period: 2023  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`  
Assessment: factual evidence only; no score assigned.

## 1. Orientation

The Philippines had no nuclear-weapons arsenal in 2023 and no operating nuclear power reactor. Its principal catastrophic-risk exposures were therefore indirect:

- possible escalation involving China, the United States, and the Philippines in the South China Sea;
- nuclear-energy development, including possible revival of the Bataan Nuclear Power Plant;
- cyberattacks against government and critical infrastructure;
- biological, AI, and other dual-use risks associated with expanding digital and scientific capacity;
- natural disasters and climate-related infrastructure vulnerability.

Marcos held formal authority as president and commander-in-chief. He could approve national-security strategies, direct executive agencies, sign legislation, appoint officials, shape alliance policy, and authorize crisis responses. His influence was constrained by Congress, courts, constitutional commissions, the military and coast guard bureaucracy, local governments, treaty obligations, and dependence on the United States, China, Japan, ASEAN, and international technical bodies.

The strongest evidence indicates that Marcos adopted a formal national-security and cybersecurity planning framework, maintained IAEA safeguards commitments, and pursued deterrence and alliance-building while publicly avoiding claims that routine maritime incidents automatically triggered war. However, implementation evidence for these strategies in 2023 is limited. There is no evidence that the Philippines acquired nuclear weapons, pursued a weapons program, or used nuclear threats to shield aggression. The main unresolved concern is whether alliance expansion and public confrontation with China reduced or increased escalation risk.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 1B.1 Reducing existential risk | Partially evidenced | WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-03 |
| 1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric | No direct nuclear rhetoric located; indirect crisis rhetoric was generally restrained | WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04 |
| 1B.3 Safe nuclear control | Mostly not applicable; no weapons arsenal. Civilian nuclear governance requires further inspection | WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-06 |
| 1B.4 Arms control and inspections | Existing NPT safeguards and Additional Protocol remained in force | WEB-1B-05 |
| 1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression | No evidence of nuclear capability or nuclear cover for aggression | WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-07 |
| 1B.6 Preventing proliferation | No evidence of proliferation activity; safeguards framework remained operative | WEB-1B-05 |
| 1B.7 Independent risk expertise | DICT strategy used technical and interagency structures; nuclear expert independence not established | WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-06 |
| 1B.8 Crisis de-escalation | Mixed: alliance strengthening and public exposure of Chinese actions coexisted with avoidance of automatic treaty escalation | WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04 |
| 1B.9 Other catastrophic risks | Cybersecurity planning was formalized, but major implementation gaps remained | WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-08 |
| 1B.10 Lasting risk posture | Cannot yet be established for 2023; policy architecture improved, but operational outcomes and durability remain insufficiently documented | WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-08 |

## 3. Evidence index

### WEB-1B-01 — National Security Policy 2023–2028 adopted

**Claim.** The Marcos administration adopted a National Security Policy for 2023–2028, creating a formal framework for national security during the target period.

**Why it matters.** A national strategy is evidence of formal risk prioritization, but not proof of implementation or improved outcomes.

**Source.** Presidential Communications Office, “PBBM Approves Adoption of National Security Policy 2023–2028,” 2023. [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-approves-adoption-of-national-security-policy-2023-2028).

**Locator.** Official announcement and policy adoption.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for approval and adoption; lower for implementation by the National Security Council, armed forces, coast guard, and other agencies.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The strategy’s existence does not establish that it contained effective safeguards against escalation or that agencies had sufficient resources to execute it. The linked page was not retrievable in this pass, so the underlying policy text should be opened directly.

**Independent support.** The supplied catalogue identifies the National Security Policy as an official 2023–2028 strategy; the DICT cybersecurity plan describes itself as aligned with the administration’s development and security strategies.

### WEB-1B-02 — National Cybersecurity Plan created a formal critical-infrastructure framework

**Claim.** The Department of Information and Communications Technology’s National Cybersecurity Plan 2023–2028 established a risk-based framework for protecting government networks, critical information infrastructure, national CERTs, incident response, threat databases, submarine cables, and cybersecurity workforce capacity.

**Why it matters.** Cyberattacks against government and critical infrastructure are potentially systemic risks. The plan shows an attempt to build preventive and response capacity.

**Source.** Department of Information and Communications Technology, *National Cybersecurity Plan 2023–2028*, February 2024, prepared through a 2023 consultation process. [Source](https://cms-cdn.e.gov.ph/DICT/pdf/NCSP-2023-2028-FINAL-DICT.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. ii–v, 1, 6–7, 13–15, 19–26. The plan states that the public draft was released in May 2023 and that consultations generated more than thirty position papers.

**Period status.** Target-period policy development, with final publication in February 2024.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to high. The plan carries a message from Marcos and is described as part of his administration’s development and security framework, but DICT led the technical drafting and implementation.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The plan itself calls its objectives ambitious and acknowledges that incident response and protection of critical infrastructure still required improvement. It proposed future legislation, standards, staffing, and institutional reforms rather than documenting completed implementation.

**Independent corroboration.** Freedom House reported that a DICT official estimated approximately 3,000 successful cyberattacks against Philippine companies and government agencies between 2020 and 2022, with 60 percent targeting government websites, and reported a 2023 breach exposing more than 1.2 million records. [Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023: Philippines*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-net/2023), lines 435–439.

### WEB-1B-03 — Marcos strengthened US and Japanese security ties while managing South China Sea escalation

**Claim.** Marcos moved the Philippines toward closer security cooperation with the United States and Japan, including expanded use and upgrading of EDCA sites, while responding publicly to Chinese pressure in disputed South China Sea waters.

**Why it matters.** Alliance arrangements can deter coercion but can also create escalation pathways in a confrontation involving China and the United States.

**Source.** Asia Maritime Transparency Initiative, “Marcos Jr. Steers Manila toward Washington and Tokyo,” 2023. [Source](https://amti.csis.org/marcos-jr-steers-manila-toward-washington-and-tokyo).

**Locator.** Analysis of the foreign-policy shift and EDCA expansion.

**Period status.** Target-period evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for diplomatic direction, alliance policy, and public framing; moderate for operational consequences, which depended on the armed forces, US forces, Japan, and local authorities.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The policy was not equivalent to abandoning relations with China. The available catalogue describes it as flexible balancing. The decision to publicize confrontations may improve deterrence and international support while increasing diplomatic friction.

**Source limitations.** AMTI is a specialist policy-analysis organization associated with a US think tank and should be balanced with Philippine, Chinese, ASEAN, and treaty records.

**Independent corroboration.** Associated Press reports documented maritime confrontations and Marcos’s effort to build regional support. [AP, “Philippines Urges Neighbors to Stand Together More Strongly Against China”](https://apnews.com/article/4fdb6358b4267d45e1ad45e6d58e6304); [AP, ship collision report](https://apnews.com/article/06e9fe0ef440aba09bc650d986d83377).

### WEB-1B-04 — Marcos did not treat every maritime incident as an automatic war trigger

**Claim.** During South China Sea incidents, Marcos publicly distinguished coercive or dangerous Chinese conduct from circumstances that would automatically activate the US-Philippines Mutual Defense Treaty.

**Why it matters.** Preserving decision space and avoiding automatic escalation is relevant to crisis-management responsibility.

**Source.** Associated Press, “Marcos: China Laser Not Enough to Activate US Defense Pact,” 2023. [Source](https://apnews.com/article/878a88b8663bf52e2e93591228312554).

**Locator.** Reported statement concerning a Chinese laser incident and treaty activation.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the public statement and diplomatic posture; lower for the underlying event.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Stronger alliance commitments and public exposure of Chinese actions could still increase the risk of miscalculation. One restrained statement cannot establish a durable crisis protocol.

**Source limitations.** AP reporting relies substantially on government statements from the parties to the dispute. Incident-level maritime records should be used to establish what happened operationally.

### WEB-1B-05 — The Philippines maintained NPT safeguards and an Additional Protocol

**Claim.** The Philippines had an IAEA safeguards agreement under the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty in force since October 16, 1974, and an Additional Protocol in force since February 26, 2010.

**Why it matters.** These are the core international verification arrangements relevant to nonproliferation and the peaceful-use commitment.

**Source.** International Atomic Energy Agency, Office of Legal Affairs, Philippines country record. [Source](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=PH).

**Locator.** “Safeguards Agreements” table: NPT safeguards agreement and Additional Protocol.

**Period status.** Inherited framework operative during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for creation; moderate for maintaining compliance and avoiding obstruction during his administration.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** A treaty record establishes formal status, not the quality of domestic implementation, inspection access, accounting, export controls, or nuclear-material security.

**Independent corroboration.** The IAEA technical document describes the Philippines as a non-nuclear-weapons state exploring future nuclear power and discusses its historical and proposed civilian nuclear program. [IAEA, *TECDOC-2086*, pp. 24–29](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/publications/PDF/TE-2086web.pdf).

### WEB-1B-06 — Nuclear-energy revival was an inherited program, not a 2023 weapons effort

**Claim.** The Philippines had no operating nuclear power plant in 2023, but the government continued to examine a civilian nuclear-energy program, including possible revival of the Bataan Nuclear Power Plant and consideration of small modular reactors. The program originated substantially before Marcos’s presidency: Duterte issued Executive Order No. 164 in 2022 endorsing a national nuclear-energy position.

**Why it matters.** Civilian nuclear development creates long-term safety, security, waste, siting, regulatory, and emergency-preparedness responsibilities, even when there is no weapons program.

**Source.** IAEA, *TECDOC-2086*, “Philippines” case study. [Source](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/publications/PDF/TE-2086web.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 24–29, especially lines 1112–1167. The source notes that BNPP was never operated because of concerns including construction flaws, seismic hazards, volcanic activity, and the Chernobyl-era context.

**Period status.** Mostly inherited baseline and broader ruler-period context; the civilian nuclear option remained relevant in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. Marcos inherited the formal nuclear-energy program and could continue, modify, or halt it. The present source does not establish a material 2023 decision by Marcos concerning construction or operation.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Nuclear power could reduce coal dependence and emissions, but the Philippines is highly exposed to earthquakes, volcanoes, typhoons, and complex archipelagic emergency logistics. The old BNPP’s history makes independent safety review essential.

**Source limitations.** The TECDOC is technical and useful for infrastructure context, but it is not a 2023 Philippine regulatory audit or a decision record.

### WEB-1B-07 — No evidence of a Philippine nuclear-weapons capability or nuclear coercion

**Claim.** No inspected source identified a Philippine nuclear-weapons arsenal, nuclear doctrine authorizing use, nuclear threats by Marcos, or use of nuclear capability to shield conventional aggression or domestic repression.

**Why it matters.** The absence of an arsenal makes lenses concerning custody, command-and-control, nuclear cover for aggression, and proliferation substantially different from those for nuclear-armed states.

**Source basis.** IAEA safeguards record; IAEA technical nuclear-program assessment; World Nuclear Association country profile candidate. [IAEA safeguards record](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=PH); [IAEA TECDOC-2086](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/publications/PDF/TE-2086web.pdf).

**Period status.** Inherited and target-period posture.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for the inherited non-nuclear status; moderate for Marcos’s continuation of the peaceful-use posture.

**Cautions.** This is a bounded negative finding, not proof that every proliferation or dual-use risk was absent. Export-control records, military procurement, intelligence assessments, and Philippine Nuclear Regulatory Commission material require further inspection.

### WEB-1B-08 — Cybersecurity threats were material, while safeguards were incomplete

**Claim.** In 2023 the Philippines faced significant cyber risk, including reported government-data exposure and continuing attacks against public and private systems. The cybersecurity plan acknowledged that government incident response and critical-infrastructure protection still required improvement.

**Why it matters.** Cyber disruption could affect finance, communications, health systems, elections, energy, and military decision-making. The combination of demonstrated vulnerability and a still-being-built institutional framework is directly relevant to catastrophic-risk responsibility.

**Sources.** Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023: Philippines*; DICT, *National Cybersecurity Plan 2023–2028*. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-net/2023); [DICT plan](https://cms-cdn.e.gov.ph/DICT/pdf/NCSP-2023-2028-FINAL-DICT.pdf).

**Locator.** Freedom House lines 435–439; DICT plan pp. 1, 6–7, 13–15, 19–26.

**Period status.** Target-year threat reporting and target-period policy development.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos’s administration adopted the policy framework, but cyber incidents involved agencies, private entities, and unidentified attackers.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The plan’s consultation process and proposed CERT, threat-intelligence, and critical-infrastructure measures are evidence of institutional response. They do not establish that the measures were funded, staffed, or effective by the end of 2023.

## 4. Information environment

Nuclear-weapons information was comparatively clear because the Philippines’ non-nuclear status and IAEA safeguards are documented in treaty records. Civilian nuclear-energy plans were less clear: several important documents concern inherited decisions, feasibility studies, or future options rather than executed 2023 actions.

Cybersecurity evidence was harder to evaluate. Government agencies reported attacks and breaches, but attribution, affected systems, remediation, and the distinction between attempted and successful attacks were often unclear. Freedom House provides useful independent reporting but draws on secondary reporting and was not itself a technical forensic investigation.

South China Sea evidence was politically contested. Philippine and Chinese official accounts differed on vessel conduct, collisions, water-cannon incidents, and legal responsibility. Independent maritime imagery, coast-guard logs, and incident-level diplomatic records are needed before making strong causal claims.

No evidence in the inspected material established that Marcos used reckless nuclear rhetoric. That conclusion should not be treated as evidence of responsible nuclear leadership; it mainly reflects the Philippines’ non-nuclear status and the absence of identified nuclear threats.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# Chapter 2B — International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security

Ruler: Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr.  
Country: Philippines  
Period: 2023  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`  
Assessment: factual evidence only; no score assigned.

## 1. Orientation and conflict-exposure gate

The Philippines was not engaged in a declared interstate war in 2023. Its principal international-security exposure was the South China Sea dispute with China, involving maritime coercion, competing territorial claims, dangerous maneuvers, and the possibility of escalation involving the United States under the Mutual Defense Treaty.

A second exposure was the long-running internal armed conflict involving the CPP-NPA-NDF, the Moro Islamic Liberation Front, the Moro National Liberation Front, and smaller armed groups. Those conflicts are primarily relevant here where Marcos pursued peace agreements, decommissioning, amnesty, or cross-border and alliance-related security choices. Ordinary policing and domestic repression belong mainly in chapter 3B.

Marcos had substantial authority over foreign policy, the armed forces, the coast guard, treaty implementation, and national peace institutions. His authority was constrained by Congress, the courts, the military and diplomatic bureaucracy, the Bangsamoro government, local governments, China, the United States, Japan, ASEAN, and pre-existing peace agreements.

The evidence shows a dual approach:

- diplomatic engagement and communication mechanisms with China;
- stronger security cooperation with the United States and Japan;
- continued public resistance to Chinese coercion;
- renewed peace-process activity with the CPP-NPA-NDF;
- implementation of the Bangsamoro peace process, including MILF decommissioning and reintegration;
- no evidence that Marcos initiated an interstate war or authorized annexationist aggression.

The central unresolved issue is whether alliance strengthening and publicizing South China Sea confrontations were proportionate deterrence or increased escalation risk. The accessible evidence supports both interpretations and does not establish a clear end-state by December 2023.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 2B.1 Peaceful alternatives | Substantially evidenced | WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-03 |
| 2B.2 Starting or prolonging war | No evidence that Marcos initiated an interstate war; inherited domestic conflicts continued | WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-05 |
| 2B.3 Honest justification for force | Mixed and incomplete; public claims emphasized defense and sovereignty, but independent review of every incident is limited | WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-06 |
| 2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection | Insufficient chapter-specific evidence; civilian-protection principles appear in the peace framework, but incident-level military conduct requires more research | WEB-2B-05 |
| 2B.5 Control of proxies and arms | Partial evidence through MILF decommissioning and reintegration; no complete alliance-transfer or coast-guard control record | WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-07 |
| 2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims | Mixed; independent media and international reporting remained active, but maritime facts were contested and official narratives dominated some incidents | WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-06 |
| 2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements | Strong evidence of peace-process activity and implementation, but no final interstate settlement with China | WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-07 |
| 2B.8 Responsible military resources | Alliance and military modernization choices were documented, but budget execution, procurement integrity, and proportionality require further inspection | WEB-2B-03, WEB-2B-08 |
| 2B.9 Accountability and remedy | Peace-process amnesty and reintegration mechanisms advanced; accountability for security-force abuses is mainly a 3B issue and remains incomplete | WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-09 |
| 2B.10 Lasting international peace | Not established for 2023. Peace institutions advanced, but South China Sea tensions remained unresolved | WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-05 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-2B-01 — Marcos opened the year with direct diplomacy and crisis-communication measures with China

**Claim and significance.** During his January 2023 state visit to China, Marcos reported that he and Xi Jinping discussed the West Philippine Sea directly, agreed that maritime disputes should not dominate the entire bilateral relationship, and agreed to establish direct communication lines at multiple levels to reduce misunderstanding and miscalculation.

This is direct evidence that Marcos pursued diplomatic alternatives before escalation and attempted to preserve crisis-management channels.

**Strongest source.** Office of the President / Presidential Communications Office, “Arrival Statement of President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. for His State Visit to the People’s Republic of China,” January 5, 2023. [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2023/01/20230105-ARRIVAL-STATEMENT-OF-PRESIDENT-FERDINAND-R.-MARCOS-JR.-FOR-HIS-STATE-VISIT-TO-THE-PEOPLES-REPUBLIC-OF-CHINA-REV.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 1–3, especially the passages stating that direct communication lines would be established “to avoid possible misunderstanding and miscalculation.”

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the diplomatic initiative and stated direction; moderate for implementation of the communication mechanism by foreign ministries and security agencies.

**Complicating evidence.** The statement is an official account and describes intended arrangements, not proof that hotlines were used effectively during later confrontations. The same visit included economic and energy cooperation, which may have created incentives for restraint but also potential leverage concerns.

**Independent corroboration.** The supplied AMTI, AP, Carnegie, and East Asia Forum candidates analyze the broader balancing strategy, though several were not retrievable in this pass.

### WEB-2B-02 — Marcos combined alliance strengthening with public resistance to Chinese coercion

**Claim and significance.** Marcos strengthened defense cooperation with the United States and Japan, including expanded EDCA access and infrastructure, while publicly opposing Chinese pressure in disputed waters. This approach sought deterrence and support for Philippine maritime rights but also increased the possibility of alliance entrapment or escalation.

**Strongest source.** Asia Maritime Transparency Initiative, “Marcos Jr. Steers Manila toward Washington and Tokyo.” [Source](https://amti.csis.org/marcos-jr-steers-manila-toward-washington-and-tokyo).

**Locator.** Analysis of the 2023 security-policy shift and EDCA expansion.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for foreign-policy direction and alliance decisions; moderate for operational effects involving the armed forces, US forces, Japan, and local authorities.

**Complicating evidence.** The strategy was not a complete break with China. Marcos’s January statement described an “independent foreign policy,” continued bilateral cooperation, and direct dispute-management mechanisms.

**Source limitations.** AMTI is a specialist policy-analysis institution associated with a US think tank. Its interpretation should be balanced with Philippine, Chinese, ASEAN, treaty, and independent maritime records.

### WEB-2B-03 — South China Sea incidents created a real but contained escalation risk

**Claim and significance.** Philippine and Chinese vessels repeatedly confronted one another in disputed waters during 2023, with reports of dangerous maneuvers, shadowing, water-cannon incidents, and a collision. These events created a plausible pathway from coercive diplomacy to armed confrontation.

**Strongest sources.**

- Associated Press, “Chinese and Philippine ship collision just the latest in a string of South China Sea confrontations.” [Source](https://apnews.com/article/06e9fe0ef440aba09bc650d986d83377)
- Associated Press, “Philippines Urges Neighbors to Stand Together More Strongly Against China.” [Source](https://apnews.com/article/4fdb6358b4267d45e1ad45e6d58e6304)
- Associated Press, “Marcos: China Laser Not Enough to Activate US Defense Pact.” [Source](https://apnews.com/article/878a88b8663bf52e2e93591228312554)

**Locator.** Incident reports and Marcos’s public position on whether a particular event activated the US defense pact.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the Philippine government’s diplomatic and public response; low to moderate for the underlying Chinese conduct, which involved another state’s actions and contested facts.

**Complicating evidence.** Marcos did not treat every dangerous encounter as automatically triggering armed conflict. The administration’s restraint on treaty activation supports a crisis-management interpretation. Conversely, publicizing incidents and expanding military access may increase deterrence while also raising escalation risks.

**Cautions.** AP reporting relies substantially on statements by the governments involved. Vessel tracking, imagery, coast-guard logs, and diplomatic notes should be used to establish the facts of individual encounters.

### WEB-2B-04 — The administration renewed peace engagement with the CPP-NPA-NDF

**Claim and significance.** The Philippine government reengaged in peace discussions with the CPP-NPA-NDF in 2023 after approximately six years without formal talks. In November 2023, the parties issued the Oslo Joint Statement committing to a principled and peaceful resolution and an end to armed struggle.

This is direct evidence that Marcos pursued a peaceful alternative to prolonging the internal armed conflict.

**Strongest source.** Office of the Presidential Adviser on Peace, Reconciliation and Unity, *State of the Philippine Peace, Reconciliation and Unity Report 2023*. [Source](https://peace.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/OPAPRU-FY-2023-Annual-Report_SOPPRU_final_compressed.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 45–50 and p. 57; lines 865–866 state that the government reengaged after six years, while lines 993–997 describe the Oslo Joint Statement.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to high. Marcos’s administration authorized and supported the renewed peace track, while OPAPRU, negotiators, local governments, and the NDF were independent participants.

**Complicating evidence.** The statement began a process rather than creating a completed ceasefire or final peace agreement. The government simultaneously maintained counterinsurgency structures under Executive Order No. 70 and NTF-ELCAC.

**Cautions.** OPAPRU is a government institution and presents the initiative as a success. The NDF’s own statements, battlefield data, local ceasefire records, and UCDP/ACLED event data should be compared.

### WEB-2B-05 — MILF decommissioning and Bangsamoro normalization advanced

**Claim and significance.** In 2023, the government and MILF continued implementing the Comprehensive Agreement on the Bangsamoro. The OPAPRU report states that Phase 3 decommissioning was completed, covering the remaining 1,294 MILF combatants, and that former combatants received livelihood, literacy, training, and reintegration assistance.

This is evidence of a concrete peace settlement being implemented rather than merely announced.

**Strongest source.** OPAPRU, *State of the Philippine Peace, Reconciliation and Unity Report 2023*. [Source](https://peace.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/OPAPRU-FY-2023-Annual-Report_SOPPRU_final_compressed.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 13–28; lines 366–368, 409–424, 472–474, and 570–597.

**Period status.** Target-year implementation of an inherited peace agreement.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos’s government supervised implementation, but the agreement and normalization process predated him and depended on the MILF, Bangsamoro institutions, Congress, local authorities, and international partners.

**Complicating evidence.** Decommissioning does not establish complete disarmament, absence of private armed groups, or full socioeconomic reintegration. The report is an official accomplishment report and requires independent verification of weapons, combatant, and assistance figures.

### WEB-2B-06 — Amnesty proclamations were used as a legal peace instrument

**Claim and significance.** On November 22, 2023, Marcos issued proclamations granting amnesty to former members of the RPM-P/RPA/ABB, CPP-NPA-NDF, MILF, and MNLF for qualifying politically motivated offenses. The House of Representatives concurred on December 12, 2023. The proclamations sought to restore legal status and support reintegration.

This is evidence of a formal legal alternative to continued prosecution and armed conflict.

**Strongest source.** OPAPRU, *State of the Philippine Peace, Reconciliation and Unity Report 2023*. [Source](https://peace.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/OPAPRU-FY-2023-Annual-Report_SOPPRU_final_compressed.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 142–152; lines 2205–2218 and 2266–2284.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for issuing the proclamations; shared with Congress and the National Amnesty Commission for legal concurrence and implementation.

**Complicating evidence.** Amnesty can encourage demobilization and reconciliation but can also weaken accountability for serious offenses if eligibility, exclusions, verification, and victim remedies are inadequate. The report records that implementation rules and procedures were still being developed.

**Cautions.** The source is an official peace-process report and does not independently evaluate whether amnesty applicants were properly screened or whether victims received remedy.

### WEB-2B-07 — Peace implementation included integration into state institutions

**Claim and significance.** The 2023 peace report states that 396 qualified MILF and MNLF applicants took oaths as Philippine National Police patrol personnel, with a further batch of 294 taking oath in December. This represented an effort to replace armed-group structures with lawful public institutions.

**Strongest source.** OPAPRU, *State of the Philippine Peace, Reconciliation and Unity Report 2023*. [Source](https://peace.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/OPAPRU-FY-2023-Annual-Report_SOPPRU_final_compressed.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 16 and 21–27; lines 402–424 and 495–500.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The national government and Bangsamoro institutions jointly managed recruitment, screening, and integration.

**Complicating evidence.** Integration can promote lawful security and trust, but it raises questions about vetting, training, command loyalty, weapons control, and treatment of former combatants and local rivals. Those safeguards were not fully documented in the inspected source.

### WEB-2B-08 — Military and alliance spending addressed a real security environment but requires proportionality review

**Claim and significance.** Local structured data supplied to the parent workflow records Philippine military expenditure of approximately US$5.15 billion in 2023, or about US$44.94 per capita. The broader policy record indicates that resources supported territorial defense, maritime surveillance, alliance interoperability, and modernization.

**Why it matters.** Military resources can provide legitimate deterrence, but large or opaque commitments may also intensify regional insecurity, enrich networks, or reduce resources available for peacebuilding.

**Source.** Local SIPRI-derived fact supplied in the orientation briefing; external corroboration candidate: [SIPRI Military Expenditure Database](https://milex.sipri.org/sipri).

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos’s administration set priorities, but appropriations and procurement involved Congress, the Department of National Defense, military services, and suppliers.

**Complicating evidence.** The South China Sea dispute and archipelagic defense needs provide a credible security rationale. The inspected web sources did not establish procurement corruption, disproportionate spending, or misuse for intimidation.

**Cautions.** A spending total alone cannot establish whether resources were effective, lawful, or appropriately allocated. Budget execution, contracts, audits, and capability delivery require separate inspection.

### WEB-2B-09 — International and domestic scrutiny remained available but contested

**Claim and significance.** The Philippines retained independent media, international reporting, constitutional commissions, congressional oversight, and peace-process transparency mechanisms. At the same time, government and belligerent narratives competed over maritime incidents, insurgency, and security policy, making independent verification difficult.

**Strongest sources.**

- [Freedom House, Philippines 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)
- [Human Rights Watch, “Philippines: Marcos Failing on Rights”](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/06/28/philippines-marcos-failing-rights)
- [CHR 2023 Annual Accomplishment Report](https://cdn1.chr.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/06100614/CHR-2023-Annual-Accomplishment-Report.pdf)
- [OPAPRU 2023 report](https://peace.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/OPAPRU-FY-2023-Annual-Report_SOPPRU_final_compressed.pdf)

**Locator.** Human-rights and media-freedom assessments; OPAPRU sections on joint communications, local peace dialogues, and reporting.

**Period status.** Target-year and inherited information environment.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for the administration’s access and disclosure practices; lower for broader media polarization and inherited intimidation patterns.

**Complicating evidence.** The existence of independent scrutiny does not mean all conflict claims were independently verified. Official peace-process reports may overstate success, while advocacy organizations may emphasize abuses and risks.

## 4. Information environment and evidence limitations

The strongest evidence was available for:

- formal peace proclamations;
- peace-process structures;
- decommissioning and reintegration programs;
- Marcos’s own diplomatic statements;
- official alliance and security-policy announcements.

The most difficult evidence concerned:

- the precise facts and legal significance of individual South China Sea encounters;
- whether Chinese conduct was coercive, accidental, or proportionate to competing claims;
- whether alliance expansion reduced or increased war risk;
- battlefield casualties and civilian harm in the CPP-NPA conflict;
- the effectiveness and impartiality of amnesty screening;
- military procurement integrity and budget execution.

Official Philippine sources are strong for what Marcos ordered, signed, or publicly said. They are weaker for proving implementation, impartiality, civilian protection, or causal success. Chinese sources and Philippine government accounts should be compared with maritime imagery, coast-guard records, diplomatic notes, and independent reporting.

## 6. Source counts and research gaps

- Candidate sources inspected: 16.
- Accepted evidence sources: 8.
- Corroborating sources: 5.
- Access-blocked or not fully extractable: 7.
- Developed evidence records: 9.
- No evidence found that Marcos initiated an interstate war.
- Strongest positive evidence: diplomatic engagement with China, crisis-communication intent, renewed CPP-NPA-NDF talks, MILF decommissioning, and formal amnesty.
- Strongest unresolved risk: whether expanded US/Japan security cooperation and public maritime confrontation produced net deterrence or greater escalation exposure.
- Main remaining gaps: incident-level maritime verification, military procurement and budget execution, civilian-harm data, peace-process independent monitoring, and amnesty screening and victim-remedy records.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Chapter 3B — Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection

Ruler: Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr.  
Country: Philippines  
Period: 2023  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`  
Assessment: factual evidence only; no score assigned.

## 1. Orientation

Marcos was president and commander-in-chief throughout 2023. He controlled national executive agencies, the Philippine National Police, armed forces, intelligence bodies, prosecutors, and national security institutions, but implementation was mediated by courts, constitutional commissions, local governments, provincial political networks, Congress, and the security bureaucracy.

The inherited baseline was severe: Duterte-era drug-war killings, red-tagging, disappearances, attacks on journalists and activists, and weak accountability. During 2023, the evidence shows some institutional restraint and oversight—especially through the Commission on Human Rights, courts, and investigations—but no decisive break from the inherited security model.

The central physical-safety concerns were:

- continuing drug-related killings and alleged police abuses;
- arbitrary arrests and detention of activists and critics;
- red-tagging by security-linked officials and agencies;
- alleged enforced disappearance and military custody of environmental activists Jonila Castro and Jhed Tamano;
- continuing danger to journalists and human-rights defenders;
- police use of force against protests;
- continuing armed conflict and violence in insurgency-affected areas.

The evidence does not establish that Marcos personally ordered particular killings or disappearances. It does establish that these events occurred under his administration, that national agencies were implicated in several allegations, and that he retained authority to rescind policies, discipline officials, support investigations, and protect victims. Evidence of remedy was partial and uneven.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 3B.1 Protection from state abuse | Adverse evidence of continuing killings, disappearances, arbitrary detention, and torture allegations; limited remedy | WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-03 |
| 3B.2 Control of coercive forces | Mixed; security forces were implicated in abuses, while some investigations and judicial remedies operated | WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04 |
| 3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred | Red-tagging by officials and security-linked bodies exposed activists, journalists, and critics to physical danger | WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-05 |
| 3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight | The CHR investigated, searched facilities, assisted victims, and processed cases; courts issued protective writs | WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04 |
| 3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers | Anti-Terrorism Act powers and NTF-ELCAC structures remained available; misuse concerns persisted | WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-06 |
| 3B.6 Safety without state terror | Drug-related killings reportedly declined from Duterte-era levels but continued; broader insecurity and impunity remained | WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-07 |
| 3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups | CHR programs assisted children, women, detainees, defenders, and other vulnerable groups, but serious risks remained | WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-08 |
| 3B.8 Safe protest and dissent | Protest remained possible, but permits, police violence, red-tagging, and criminal cases created physical and legal risks | WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-06 |
| 3B.9 Restrained crisis response | Castro–Tamano response showed both active oversight and official denial of alleged abduction; the government’s handling remained contested | WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04 |
| 3B.10 Lasting domestic safety | Not established. Some institutions acted protectively, but no demonstrable durable break from impunity was shown | WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-04 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-3B-01 — Drug-related killings and security-force abuses continued

**Claim and significance.** Human Rights Watch reported that police and state agents continued drug-related killings, arbitrary arrests, enforced disappearances, torture allegations, and mistreatment of activists and critics during Marcos’s first year. HRW stated that killings occurred at a lower rate than under Duterte but that Marcos had not formally rescinded the policies underpinning the drug war or established meaningful accountability.

This is the clearest evidence that the administration did not fully break with the inherited coercive model.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch, “Philippines: Marcos Failing on Rights,” June 28, 2023. [Source](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/06/28/philippines-marcos-failing-rights).

**Locator.** The report states that police and agents continued drug-war killings; cites 336 drug-related killings since Marcos took office; notes only three police convictions across thousands of cases; and reports continuing arbitrary arrests, disappearances, torture allegations, and red-tagging.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023 and the first year of Marcos’s presidency, while also describing inherited Duterte-era practices.

**Ruler attribution.** High for policy continuity, failure to rescind operative orders, and failure to direct stronger investigations; lower for individual killings absent evidence of presidential command.

**Complicating evidence.** The administration claimed a more rehabilitation-focused and less violent anti-drug approach. The reported lower rate of killings is relevant contrary evidence, but it does not establish adequate protection or remedy.

**Source limitations.** HRW is an independent advocacy organization. Its claims should be reconciled with CHR, PNP, prosecutorial, judicial, and local case records.

### WEB-3B-02 — The Commission on Human Rights recorded serious violations and provided oversight

**Claim and significance.** The Philippine Commission on Human Rights recorded 93 alleged extrajudicial-killing cases, 35 torture cases, and 12 enforced-disappearance cases in 2023. It also reported that the right to life was the largest category of civil-rights violation in its complaints caseload.

At the same time, the CHR reported disposing of 3,055 of 6,544 cases handled, conducting 951 detention-facility visits, assisting 4,474 persons deprived of liberty, deploying 115 quick-response operations, and providing legal or rehabilitation support.

**Strongest source.** Commission on Human Rights of the Philippines, *2023 Annual Accomplishment Report*. [Source](https://cdn1.chr.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/06100614/CHR-2023-Annual-Accomplishment-Report.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 5–9; especially lines 230–273 and 302–347.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The CHR was constitutionally independent, so its cases describe the national safety environment rather than proving presidential direction. Its continued operation and access to victims are relevant to the administration’s oversight environment.

**Complicating evidence.** The same report documents substantial protective and remedial activity, including legal assistance, detention visits, rapid response, victim support, and policy advice.

**Cautions.** CHR figures are recorded allegations and cases, not final judicial findings. Underreporting is likely where victims fear retaliation, and the annual report emphasizes institutional accomplishments.

### WEB-3B-03 — Castro and Tamano’s alleged disappearance exposed security-force accountability problems

**Claim and significance.** Environmental defenders Jonila Castro and Jhed Tamano disappeared in Bataan on September 2, 2023. The CHR conducted an independent investigation and searched 15 military camps and police detention facilities. Government representatives later said the two had voluntarily surrendered to the military and characterized the incident as a hoax. Castro and Tamano subsequently sought writs of amparo and habeas data from the Supreme Court.

The case is important because it involved alleged disappearance, military custody, official denial, public presentation by NTF-ELCAC, and judicial protection.

**Strongest sources.**

- Commission on Human Rights statement, September 23, 2023. [Source](https://chr.gov.ph/statement-of-the-commission-on-human-rights-on-the-case-of-the-resurfaced-missing-environmental-activists/)
- Supreme Court E-Library, *Castro and Tamano v. Dela Cruz et al.*, G.R. No. 269249. [Source](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69252)

**Locator.** CHR statement: September 2 disappearance, 15 facility searches, and September 15 government account. Supreme Court decision: ¶¶ 50–55 and 75–77; the Court issued the writs at ¶¶ 292–295.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to high for national-agency involvement and the administration’s response; not sufficient to establish that Marcos ordered an abduction. The case names personnel of the 70th Infantry Battalion, Philippine National Police, NSC, and NTF-ELCAC.

**Complicating evidence.** Government agencies denied abduction and said the activists voluntarily surrendered. The CHR’s search activity and the Supreme Court’s issuance of protective writs show that independent oversight remained available.

**Cautions.** The Supreme Court decision issued protective writs but does not by itself constitute a final finding that the military abducted the activists. The case requires later judicial and investigative follow-up.

### WEB-3B-04 — Courts and the CHR provided meaningful but incomplete remedies

**Claim and significance.** The Supreme Court issued writs of amparo and habeas data for Castro and Tamano, directing respondents to comply with formal return procedures. The CHR independently investigated the disappearance allegations and searched security facilities.

This demonstrates that victims could invoke independent institutions, even when state agencies were themselves implicated.

**Strongest sources.** [Supreme Court E-Library decision](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69252); [CHR statement](https://chr.gov.ph/statement-of-the-commission-on-human-rights-on-the-case-of-the-resurfaced-missing-environmental-activists/).

**Locator.** Supreme Court ¶¶ 134–135 and 292–295; CHR statement dated September 23, 2023.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The institutions were constitutionally or judicially independent, but the executive was responsible for cooperating with their processes and disciplining officials if misconduct were established.

**Complicating evidence.** Protective writs are not the same as final findings, convictions, compensation, or institutional reform. The available record does not establish a completed remedy by the end of 2023.

### WEB-3B-05 — Red-tagging created physical risks for activists, journalists, and critics

**Claim and significance.** Human Rights Watch, Freedom House, and other monitors reported that government, military, police, and NTF-ELCAC officials accused civil-society groups, journalists, opposition figures, teachers, and activists of communist or terrorist affiliation. Red-tagging can expose targets to surveillance, arbitrary arrest, enforced disappearance, and attacks by security forces or unidentified gunmen.

**Strongest sources.**

- Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2023: Philippines*. [Source](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/philippines)
- Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines*. [Source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** HRW lines 62–70; Freedom House lines 71–75, 135–150.

**Period status.** Primarily 2022–2023 context, with continuing practice under Marcos.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. NTF-ELCAC, police, and military actors were national institutions operating under executive authority, but not every individual statement or local act can be attributed directly to Marcos.

**Complicating evidence.** Officials argued that the labels identified genuine insurgent support or national-security threats. However, credible evidence of physical danger and the lack of transparent evidentiary standards complicate that justification.

**Cautions.** Red-tagging is an interpretive category; individual cases should be checked against official records, court findings, and direct statements. The physical-safety link is strongest where a target was later arrested, disappeared, attacked, or killed.

### WEB-3B-06 — Anti-terrorism and protest controls created continuing coercive risks

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House reported that the Anti-Terrorism Act gave the state broad powers, including warrantless arrest and detention of persons designated as terrorists by the Anti-Terrorism Council, which is appointed by the president. It also reported that permits were required for rallies and that police sometimes used violence to disperse antigovernment protests.

These powers and practices affected the physical safety of protesters, activists, and organizations, not merely abstract civil liberties.

**Strongest source.** Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines*. [Source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023).

**Locator.** Lines 140–150 and 171–178.

**Period status.** The legal framework was inherited and remained operative in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos did not enact the Anti-Terrorism Act, but his administration retained the executive bodies and powers created by it and could seek amendment, restraint, or repeal.

**Complicating evidence.** The Supreme Court invalidated an overbroad definition of terrorism in 2021, and some courts demonstrated independence. These are important institutional constraints and corrective mechanisms.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is an independent monitoring organization but combines legal analysis, reporting, and country-level judgment. Specific protest incidents and arrests require case-level records.

### WEB-3B-07 — Journalists and media workers remained exposed to lethal and coercive violence

**Claim and significance.** The Committee to Protect Journalists reported that three Filipino journalists—Percival Mabasa, Renato Blanco, and Federico Gempesaw—were murdered in connection with their work during the first year of Marcos’s presidency. CPJ also recorded 53 press-freedom violations, including red-tagging, cyber-libel, and physical attacks, during the same period.

Journalist safety is directly relevant to domestic physical security because killings and threats can deter reporting on corruption, security abuses, and political violence.

**Strongest source.** Committee to Protect Journalists, “In Marcos Jr.’s Philippines, milder tone belies harsh media reality,” May 18, 2023. [Source](https://cpj.org/2023/05/in-marcos-jr-s-philippines-milder-tone-belies-harsh-media-reality/).

**Locator.** Lines 539–551.

**Period status.** First year of Marcos’s presidency, including much of 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The killings were not shown to have been ordered by Marcos, but the national government was responsible for investigation, witness protection, and correcting impunity patterns.

**Complicating evidence.** CPJ described a milder presidential tone toward media than Duterte’s, and a suspected gunman in the Mabasa case was arrested. However, alleged masterminds remained at large and CPJ found no clear reversal of impunity.

**Cautions.** CPJ uses a work-related classification and may distinguish confirmed from suspected cases. Individual criminal responsibility must be determined by courts.

### WEB-3B-08 — Vulnerable groups received formal CHR support, but exposure to violence persisted

**Claim and significance.** The CHR reported assistance to children, women, human-rights defenders, Indigenous people, LGBTQIA+ people, persons deprived of liberty, lawyers, doctors, and other vulnerable groups. It provided financial and rehabilitation assistance to 765 individuals and legal assistance to 5,933 clients.

These programs are positive evidence of institutional protection, but their existence does not establish that the underlying threats were reduced.

**Strongest source.** CHR, *2023 Annual Accomplishment Report*. [Source](https://cdn1.chr.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/06100614/CHR-2023-Annual-Accomplishment-Report.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 7–9; lines 317–347.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The CHR is independent, and the evidence mainly concerns its own work rather than presidential direction. It is relevant to the national remedy environment.

**Complicating evidence.** The same report recorded 93 alleged extrajudicial killings, 35 torture cases, 12 enforced disappearances, and 829 gender-related cases.

**Cautions.** Assistance numbers measure institutional activity, not prevention or successful remedy. They also do not show equitable coverage across all regions.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The Philippines retained functioning courts, an independent constitutional human-rights commission, active local civil society, international monitoring, and investigative media. These institutions generated evidence that would be unavailable in a fully closed security system.

However, information was difficult to obtain for the most serious abuses because:

- military and police agencies disputed disappearances and described alleged victims as voluntary surrenderers;
- red-tagging created risks for victims, witnesses, journalists, and investigators;
- police and government agencies stopped or reduced publication of some drug-war statistics;
- official and independent estimates of killings differed substantially;
- local political and security networks obscured chains of command;
- many killings were committed by unidentified gunmen, making state responsibility difficult to prove;
- journalists and activists faced harassment, cyber-libel, threats, and physical attack.

The evidence is strongest for documented institutional actions, such as CHR searches, Supreme Court writs, official reports, and published allegations. It is weaker for assigning personal responsibility to Marcos for individual killings or disappearances without orders, appointments, funding, command evidence, or proof of notice followed by deliberate non-action.

- Detention and prison inspection records, including torture-prevention visits and conditions.
- 2023 protest arrest, permit, and police-use-of-force records.
- UCDP, PTS, CIRIGHTS, V-Dem, and Dahas event-level data for 2023.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected: 18.
- Accepted evidence sources: 12.
- Corroborating sources: 6.
- Access-blocked or not fully extractable: 6.
- Developed evidence records: 8.
- Strongest adverse evidence: continuing drug-related killings, red-tagging, arbitrary detention, disappearance allegations, and journalist danger.
- Strongest positive evidence: CHR investigations and assistance, Supreme Court protection, some prosecutions and institutional checks, and a lower reported rate of drug-related killings than under Duterte.
- Main unresolved questions: national command responsibility, actual 2023 police death totals, final outcome of the Castro–Tamano case, NTF-ELCAC accountability, protest-policing records, and whether any security-force reforms materially reduced abuse.
