--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Chapter 4B — Political Freedom versus Authoritarian Rule

Ruler: Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr.  
Country: Philippines  
Period: 2023  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`  
Assessment: factual evidence only; no score assigned.

## 1. Orientation

Marcos held the presidency throughout 2023 under the 1987 Constitution. He exercised substantial executive authority, but Congress, the courts, COMELEC, the Commission on Human Rights, local governments, media organizations, civil society, and political factions retained meaningful autonomy.

The Philippines remained electorally competitive and politically pluralist, but not an even or fully secure democracy. Freedom House described the country as “Partly Free,” citing open elections alongside vote buying, political dynasties, disinformation, political violence, criminal cases against critics, red-tagging, media attacks, and weak anticorruption institutions. [Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023).

There was no presidential or congressional alternation test in 2023: Marcos’s six-year presidential term began in 2022, and the next national legislative elections were scheduled for 2025. The main electoral test was the October 30, 2023 barangay and Sangguniang Kabataan elections. COMELEC administered the elections under constitutional and statutory authority, while vote buying and election-related violence remained significant concerns.

The administration’s political-freedom record was therefore mixed:

- formal electoral institutions continued operating;
- courts sometimes constrained executive or security bodies;
- media and civil society remained active;
- but red-tagging, website blocking, cyber-libel, criminal cases, political dynasties, disinformation, and threats narrowed effective competition;
- Marcos’s administration generally adopted a milder public tone toward media than Duterte’s, but did not clearly dismantle the inherited coercive and censorial infrastructure.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 4B.1 Genuine electoral choice | Partially evidenced; elections remained competitive, but 2023 local elections faced vote buying, violence, dynastic control, and uneven resources | WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-02 |
| 4B.2 No entrenchment of power | No evidence of a 2023 term-limit or constitutional extension attempt; structural patronage and media concentration remained | WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-04 |
| 4B.3 Opposition and civic freedom | Mixed to adverse; opposition and civil society remained active but faced red-tagging, criminal cases, threats, and media pressure | WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06 |
| 4B.4 Independent checks on power | Courts, COMELEC, and CHR retained meaningful formal roles; institutional independence remained imperfect | WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-07 |
| 4B.5 Politically neutral institutions | Mixed; formal appointments and institutions continued, but dynastic and coalition influence weakened neutrality | WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-08 |
| 4B.6 Independent information and media | Adverse evidence of blocking, red-tagging, lawsuits, journalist killings, and ownership concentration; independent media still operated | WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-09 |
| 4B.7 Equal political rights | Formal rights were broad, but women, minorities, Indigenous groups, opposition regions, and poorer candidates faced unequal access | WEB-4B-03 |
| 4B.8 Term limits and transfer | No adverse 2023 evidence located; the presidential term-limit framework remained intact | WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-03 |
| 4B.9 Digital freedom and surveillance | Mixed to adverse; online political speech remained possible, but disinformation, DDoS attacks, cyber-libel, surveillance concerns, and website blocking persisted | WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-09 |
| 4B.10 Lasting democratic resilience | Not established. Electoral and judicial institutions persisted, but the administration did not demonstrably reverse entrenched media, patronage, and coercive practices | WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-07 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-4B-01 — Electoral institutions continued to operate under constitutional constraints

**Claim and significance.** The Philippine Constitution assigns election administration to COMELEC, which exercises executive, quasi-judicial, and quasi-legislative powers. In a 2023 case concerning the postponement of barangay elections, the Supreme Court emphasized that COMELEC’s authority was delegated and limited by Congress, while Congress retained broader authority over election scheduling.

This demonstrates that election administration was not legally subordinate to presidential discretion.

**Strongest source.** Supreme Court of the Philippines, *Macalintal v. COMELEC*, G.R. No. 263590, June 27, 2023. [Source](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri2023/jun2023/gr_263590_2023.html).

**Locator.** Lines 124–170, especially the discussion of COMELEC’s constitutional powers and statutory limits.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023, although the election-postponement dispute began in 2022.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos signed or implemented election-related laws through the ordinary constitutional system, but the Supreme Court and Congress supplied the decisive legal constraints.

**Complicating evidence.** Freedom House noted that COMELEC commissioners are presidential appointees and that appointment safeguards against partisanship are limited. It also cited technical and procurement problems in recent elections.

**Cautions.** The case concerns the legal allocation of powers, not the overall quality of the 2023 local elections or the independence of every COMELEC decision.

### WEB-4B-02 — The October 2023 barangay and SK elections were competitive but exposed to vote buying and violence

**Claim and significance.** COMELEC scheduled and administered the October 30, 2023 barangay and Sangguniang Kabataan elections. COMELEC published election calendars, candidate data, and election statistics. Marcos publicly directed police and COMELEC to respond to reported vote buying. Independent monitoring and police reporting nevertheless recorded vote-buying allegations and election-related violence.

This supports a finding of continued electoral competition but imperfect equality and security.

**Sources.**

- COMELEC, Resolution No. 10905 and 2023 BSKE materials. [Source](https://www.comelec.gov.ph/?r=2023BSKE)
- Presidential Communications Office, “PBBM: Gov’t to do its part in addressing vote buying,” October 30, 2023. [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-govt-to-do-its-part-in-addressing-vote-buying-during-conduct-of-barangay-sk-polls/)
- Center for Media Freedom and Responsibility, “Reports on widespread violence belie ‘generally peaceful’ 2023 elections.” [Source](https://cmfr-phil.org/media-ethics-responsibility/journalism-review/reports-on-widespread-violence-belie-generally-peaceful-2023-elections/)

**Locator.** COMELEC’s October 30 election calendar; PCO statement on police and COMELEC response; CMFR’s compilation of 59 political-violence incidents before the election and reported police figures.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos had national executive and police authority and publicly directed enforcement, but most incidents involved local candidates, local officials, private armed groups, and decentralized political networks.

**Complicating evidence.** COMELEC and police described the elections as generally peaceful in many areas, and official systems for reporting vote buying and election offenses existed.

**Cautions.** Local election violence cannot automatically be attributed to Marcos. It becomes more relevant to his record where national security forces failed to act, national officials used public resources, or central institutions obstructed investigation.

### WEB-4B-03 — Political competition was structurally constrained by dynasties, patronage, and concentrated resources

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House reported that elections were formally open but that political dynasties, patronage, high campaign costs, vote buying, and access to state resources gave established elites major advantages. It described the president as dominant in policymaking and noted that a few dozen families held disproportionate political authority.

This is evidence of a competitive but unequal political system rather than a closed authoritarian regime.

**Strongest source.** Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines*. [Source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023).

**Locator.** Lines 29–31; 63–70; 79–87; 93–108.

**Period status.** 2022–2023 country-level assessment, with structural conditions continuing during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to low for the underlying dynastic system; moderate for presidential reliance on coalition and patronage networks. The evidence does not show Marcos creating the system in 2023.

**Complicating evidence.** Multiple parties, fluid coalitions, independent candidates, active civil society, and competitive campaigns remained possible.

**Cautions.** Country-level structural findings should not be converted into direct claims that Marcos personally rigged elections or ordered patronage in a particular contest.

### WEB-4B-04 — The administration did not attempt a 2023 term-limit extension

**Claim and significance.** No inspected 2023 source documents a Marcos proposal, decree, or legislative effort to extend his six-year presidential term, amend succession rules for his personal benefit, or cancel the constitutional transfer framework.

This is a relevant absence for the anti-entrenchment and succession lenses, but it is not evidence that all other institutions were protected.

**Source basis.** Freedom House’s description of the constitutional presidential term and the 2023 COMELEC/Supreme Court record. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023); [Supreme Court, G.R. No. 263590](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri2023/jun2023/gr_263590_2023.html).

**Period status.** Target-year constitutional posture.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for Marcos’s compliance with the existing term framework.

**Complicating evidence.** The absence of an extension attempt does not resolve concerns about dynastic succession, party dominance, media concentration, or the use of coercive institutions against critics.

**Cautions.** Negative findings are bounded: they show no such act was located in the inspected sources, not that no proposal or private discussion existed.

### WEB-4B-05 — Red-tagging and legal pressure narrowed effective opposition and civic freedom

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House and Human Rights Watch reported continuing red-tagging of activists, journalists, opposition figures, teachers, NGOs, and critics by government, military, police, and NTF-ELCAC-linked officials. Red-tagging associated targets with communist or terrorist activity and could expose them to surveillance, arrest, physical attack, or killing.

The Reuters Institute documented that alternative news site Bulatlat remained blocked under a government order issued shortly before Marcos took office and that the Marcos administration had not lifted the order by August 2023.

**Sources.**

- [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)
- [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2023: Philippines](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/philippines)
- [Reuters Institute, “The year-long fight of a Filipino news site against red-tagging and state censorship”](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/news/year-long-fight-filipino-news-site-against-red-tagging-and-state-censorship)

**Locator.** Freedom House lines 71–75, 135–150; HRW lines 62–70 and 71–81; Reuters Institute lines 184–204 and 216–234.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023 but includes orders inherited from the Duterte administration.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to high for the administration’s failure to lift the blocking order and for national-agency conduct continuing under its authority; lower for individual red-tagging statements without evidence of presidential direction.

**Complicating evidence.** The administration’s public tone toward media was described as milder than Duterte’s, and Bulatlat remained able to operate through mirrors, donations, and court proceedings.

**Cautions.** The blocking order originated before Marcos took office. Attribution should therefore distinguish inherited action from the administration’s decision to maintain, challenge, or ignore it.

### WEB-4B-06 — Independent media survived, but journalists faced violence, lawsuits, and censorship pressure

**Claim and significance.** CPJ reported 53 press-freedom violations during Marcos’s first year and the work-related murders of three journalists. Reuters Institute documented attacks, red-tagging, legal pressure, and the continued blocking of Bulatlat. RSF described the Philippines as one of the world’s most dangerous countries for journalists and noted continuing red-tagging and media ownership concentration.

Independent journalism remained possible, but the cost and physical risk of criticism were high.

**Sources.**

- [Committee to Protect Journalists, “In Marcos Jr.’s Philippines, milder tone belies harsh media reality”](https://cpj.org/2023/05/in-marcos-jr-s-philippines-milder-tone-belies-harsh-media-reality/)
- [Reuters Institute, Bulatlat report](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/news/year-long-fight-filipino-news-site-against-red-tagging-and-state-censorship)
- [Reporters Without Borders, Philippines country profile](https://rsf.org/en/country/philippines)

**Locator.** CPJ lines 539–551; Reuters Institute lines 193–234; RSF lines 380–398 and 392.

**Period status.** First year of Marcos’s presidency, including 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The evidence does not show Marcos ordering journalist attacks, but the administration was responsible for investigations, media-security policy, website blocking decisions, and correction of inherited abuse.

**Complicating evidence.** Private media remained vibrant and outspoken, and CPJ described a less openly antagonistic presidential tone than under Duterte.

**Cautions.** Individual journalist killings require separate criminal investigation. RSF’s current country profile contains later retrospective material; the 2023 Reuters and CPJ records are stronger for the target period.

### WEB-4B-07 — Courts retained capacity to constrain executive and security institutions

**Claim and significance.** The Supreme Court’s 2023 COMELEC decision clarified limits on election postponement powers. Other judicial records in the supplied catalogue include protection against red-tagging and security abuses. Freedom House also reported that courts had invalidated an overbroad section of the Anti-Terrorism Act and sometimes convicted police and public officials.

This is evidence that judicial checks continued to function, though imperfectly.

**Strongest sources.**

- Supreme Court, G.R. No. 263590. [Source](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri2023/jun2023/gr_263590_2023.html)
- Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Philippines*. [Source](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Supreme Court lines 124–170; Freedom House lines 164–178.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023 and inherited legal decisions still operative during the year.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. These are evidence of institutional constraint on the president, not necessarily of presidential support for judicial independence.

**Complicating evidence.** Freedom House also cited judicial inefficiency, intimidation, corruption, vacancies, and politicized decisions. Courts constrained some executive actions but did not eliminate political pressure.

**Cautions.** The Supreme Court’s institutional independence should be judged through a broader sample of appointments, cases, removals, and compliance, not one decision.

### WEB-4B-08 — Political appointments and coalition networks raised neutrality concerns

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House reported that COMELEC commissioners are presidential appointees and that safeguards against partisan appointments are limited. It also described strong presidential dominance, dynastic politics, and coalition fluidity. RSF reported that Marcos’s cousin Martin Romualdez was Speaker of the House and that Romualdez’s media company entered a joint venture with ABS-CBN’s radio business in 2023.

These facts raise concerns about the separation between political power, appointments, legislative leadership, and media influence.

**Sources.**

- [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)
- [RSF Philippines country profile](https://rsf.org/en/country/philippines)

**Locator.** Freedom House lines 54–58 and 93–108; RSF lines 380–392.

**Period status.** Target-year and structural ruler-period evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The Speaker was a political relative and coalition ally, but media ownership and appointments do not alone prove presidential control or unlawful capture.

**Complicating evidence.** COMELEC and courts retained independent formal authority, and multiple media organizations continued criticizing the government.

**Cautions.** The RSF source is a current country profile containing retrospective material. The 2023 joint venture should be verified through corporate records and contemporary reporting.

### WEB-4B-09 — Digital political space remained open but vulnerable to disinformation and state-linked pressure

**Claim and significance.** Freedom House reported that Filipinos could generally express political views online, but faced organized disinformation, red-tagging, harassment, criminal libel, cyberattacks, and state pressure. The Reuters Institute documented the effects of website blocking and social-media restrictions on Bulatlat.

This suggests a hybrid information environment: no comprehensive internet shutdown, but substantial pressure that could distort political competition and investigative reporting.

**Sources.**

- [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023: Philippines](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-net/2023)
- [Reuters Institute, Bulatlat report](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/news/year-long-fight-filipino-news-site-against-red-tagging-and-state-censorship)
- [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/philippines/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Freedom House online-freedom report; Reuters Institute lines 193–234; Freedom House political-freedom report lines 79–82 and 134–135.

**Period status.** Target-year and inherited digital-governance context.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Some blocking and harassment originated before Marcos, but the administration did not clearly dismantle the framework and national institutions remained involved.

**Complicating evidence.** Independent online outlets, fact-checkers, civil-society organizations, and social-media users continued to publish criticism.

**Cautions.** Platform moderation, state censorship, cyberattacks, and political disinformation are distinct mechanisms and should not be merged without incident-level evidence.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Political-freedom evidence was relatively accessible from courts, COMELEC, Freedom House, CPJ, RSF, Reuters Institute, and local media. The main limitations were attribution and timing.

Important distinctions:

- Marcos won the presidency in 2022; 2023 did not test presidential alternation.
- The October 2023 local elections were competitive, but local violence and vote buying cannot automatically be attributed to the national president.
- The Bulatlat blocking order began before Marcos took office; the relevant Marcos-era question is whether his administration maintained, lifted, or challenged it.
- Red-tagging was carried out by a mixture of national officials, security institutions, political allies, and local actors.
- Media ownership concentration can reduce pluralism without proving direct censorship.
- Disinformation campaigns during the 2022 election are relevant as inherited context, but they should not be treated as 2023 presidential conduct without new evidence.
- Official claims that media or activists were security threats require independent evidence; otherwise they remain state representations rather than established facts.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected: 20.
- Accepted evidence sources: 12.
- Corroborating sources: 6.
- Access-blocked or not fully extractable: 7.
- Developed evidence records: 9.
- Strongest positive evidence: functioning elections, COMELEC’s formal autonomy, active independent media and civil society, and courts that retained capacity to constrain government institutions.
- Strongest adverse evidence: dynastic and patronage dominance, vote buying, election violence, red-tagging, website blocking, criminal and administrative pressure on critics, journalist danger, and concentrated political-media networks.
- Main unresolved questions: actual 2023 election-offense outcomes, Marcos-era appointment practices, whether the Bulatlat blocking order was actively maintained by his administration, digital surveillance procurement, and whether security-linked intimidation materially changed political participation.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Chapter 5B — Economic Well-Being and Prosperity

Ruler: Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr.  
Country: Philippines  
Period: 2023  
Prompt version: `chapter_research_prompt_v2`  
Lens presentation: `layered_lenses_v1`  
Assessment: factual evidence only; no score assigned.

## 1. Orientation

Marcos held executive authority over economic policy, agriculture, infrastructure, public investment, appointments, and budget execution. His administration operated alongside an independent central bank, Congress, the Department of Finance, the National Economic and Development Authority, the Philippine Statistics Authority, and international lenders.

The economic baseline was a post-pandemic recovery affected by global inflation, high interest rates, weaker trade, Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, energy-market volatility, and the El Niño weather pattern. The Philippines grew in 2023, but inflation and food prices—especially rice—put pressure on households. Growth was driven mainly by domestic consumption, services, remittances, tourism, and business-process outsourcing rather than a clearly demonstrated productivity transformation.

The record is mixed:

- macroeconomic stability was broadly preserved;
- fiscal consolidation and investment-oriented reforms continued;
- poverty and employment conditions improved in some measures;
- but inflation, rice prices, low-productivity work, inequality, and food-supply weaknesses remained serious;
- the rice-price ceiling was a visible but temporary intervention;
- the Maharlika Investment Fund was established despite substantial governance and economic criticism;
- the evidence for high-quality infrastructure delivery and durable productivity gains remains incomplete.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Disposition | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 5B.1 Broad and sustainable prosperity | Mixed; growth and investment reforms continued, but distribution, productivity, and food-price problems remained | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02 |
| 5B.2 Qualified economic leadership | Partial evidence of professional economic management; appointment and delegation records require deeper inspection | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-03 |
| 5B.3 Macroeconomic stability | Broadly positive but constrained by inflation, debt, interest rates, and trade weakness | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-04 |
| 5B.4 Fair economic rules | Positive formal reforms for foreign investment and PPPs; implementation and distributional effects remain uncertain | WEB-5B-01 |
| 5B.5 Resistance to economic capture | Adverse and unresolved concerns surrounding Maharlika governance and political connections | WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-06 |
| 5B.6 Productive public investment | Public investment and infrastructure remained priorities, but delivery quality and realized productivity gains require audit and project-level evidence | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-07 |
| 5B.7 Evidence and correction | Mixed; government changed the rice-price policy when conditions shifted, but official success claims require independent verification | WEB-5B-08, WEB-5B-09 |
| 5B.8 Fair distribution | Mixed to adverse; poor households were disproportionately exposed to food inflation, while low-productivity employment remained a concern | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02 |
| 5B.9 Managing economic shocks | Active but mixed; rice controls, subsidies, transfers, and fiscal/monetary measures addressed shocks, but supply and distribution weaknesses persisted | WEB-5B-08, WEB-5B-09 |
| 5B.10 Lasting economic trajectory | Not established. The economy remained stable and growing, but durable improvements in productivity, inequality, and food security were not yet demonstrated | WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02, WEB-5B-05 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-5B-01 — Growth continued, but inflation and low-quality employment constrained prosperity

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank estimated Philippine growth at approximately 5.6 percent in 2023. Growth was supported by a healthy labor market, remittances, household consumption, services, tourism, and the information-technology/business-process-outsourcing sector. At the same time, high inflation, elevated borrowing costs, weak international trade, and geopolitical uncertainty reduced private investment and household purchasing power.

The World Bank also emphasized that the labor market was shifting toward low-productivity jobs and that reforms were still needed to produce sustained job creation and poverty reduction.

**Strongest source.** World Bank, “Implementing Reforms to Boost Private Investment Key to Sustained Job Creation and Poverty Reduction in the Philippines,” December 5, 2023. [Source](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph).

**Locator.** Lines 24–38.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Marcos’s administration influenced fiscal, investment, agriculture, and development policy, but macroeconomic outcomes were also shaped by the central bank, Congress, remittances, global conditions, and private demand.

**Complicating evidence.** Growth, employment, and poverty reduction provide positive evidence. Inflation, low-productivity jobs, and weak investment provide contrary evidence against treating GDP growth as broad prosperity.

**Cautions.** The World Bank’s estimate is authoritative but macroeconomic aggregates cannot show household-level distribution without survey and price data.

### WEB-5B-02 — The World Bank identified high food-price exposure among poor households

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank reported that headline inflation averaged 7.9 percent during the first four months of 2023, the highest among ASEAN peers in its June update. It also noted that poor households suffered more than average households from high food prices and that food-price stabilization and social protection were central policy challenges.

This is important because economic well-being depends on real purchasing power, not growth alone.

**Strongest source.** World Bank, *Philippines Economic Update: Securing a Clean Energy Future*, June 2023. [Source](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/c4aa826d0cda3d9b21db1adc4f8e1787-0070062023/original/World-Bank-Philippines-Economic-Update-June-2023.pdf).

**Locator.** Executive-summary material and contents referring to inflation, food prices, employment, poverty, and shared prosperity; especially pp. 16–28.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Inflation was heavily affected by global commodity conditions and monetary policy, but the administration controlled agricultural, trade, subsidy, and food-distribution responses.

**Complicating evidence.** Inflation later eased, and the World Bank identified targeted cash transfers, food assistance, farmer subsidies, and fuel subsidies as available mitigation tools.

**Cautions.** Food-price effects varied by region, household composition, and income source. National inflation averages do not fully measure welfare loss.

### WEB-5B-03 — Investment and competition reforms aimed to improve the business environment

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank identified reforms enacted in recent years that were intended to increase investment and competition, including amendments to the Public Services Act, Retail Trade Liberalization Act, Foreign Investment Act, Renewable Energy Act implementing rules, and the new Public-Private Partnership Code.

These measures sought to make markets more open and predictable and to attract foreign capital, but their effects depended on implementation, regulation, infrastructure, and competition enforcement.

**Strongest source.** World Bank, December 5, 2023 Philippines Economic Update release. [Source](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph).

**Locator.** Lines 28–31.

**Period status.** Ruler-period policy implementation, with several reforms enacted before or during the Marcos administration.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The administration supported implementation and policy continuity, while Congress enacted legislation and regulators controlled practical effects.

**Complicating evidence.** The World Bank stated that full implementation remained necessary and that structural barriers in trade, transport, telecommunications, water, education, and health still constrained growth.

**Cautions.** Formal liberalization does not automatically produce fair competition. Project-level investment, market concentration, labor conditions, and regulatory enforcement require separate evidence.

### WEB-5B-04 — Fiscal consolidation and debt stability were maintained, but fiscal space remained constrained

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank reported that the Philippines’ fiscal deficit narrowed to 7.3 percent of GDP in 2022 and that national-government debt stabilized around 61 percent of GDP. In 2023, the World Bank continued to emphasize fiscal consolidation, stronger revenue intake, and improved spending efficiency as necessary for investor confidence and durable growth.

This indicates macroeconomic management remained broadly stable but incomplete.

**Strongest sources.**

- World Bank, June 2023 Economic Update. [Source](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/philippines/publication/highlights-of-the-philippines-economic-update-june-2023-securing-a-clean-energy-future)
- World Bank, December 2023 Economic Update. [Source](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph)

**Locator.** June update lines 10–15; December update lines 36–38.

**Period status.** Target-year and inherited fiscal context.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Fiscal policy was jointly controlled by the executive, Congress, the Bureau of the Treasury, the Department of Finance, and the central bank.

**Complicating evidence.** Debt stabilization and deficit reduction support a positive macroeconomic account. High interest rates, high debt, and continued revenue and spending challenges limited policy space.

**Cautions.** Debt ratios and deficits do not reveal distributional effects, project quality, or whether fiscal adjustment reduced essential services.

### WEB-5B-05 — The Maharlika Investment Fund created a significant governance and capture risk

**Claim and significance.** Marcos signed Republic Act No. 11954 on July 18, 2023, creating the Philippines’ first sovereign wealth fund. The administration presented it as a vehicle to optimize government financial assets, finance development, support infrastructure, attract investment, and increase growth.

The fund also created concerns about governance, public-asset risk, political influence, transparency, and the absence of a clear investment track record.

**Strongest source.** Department of Finance, “Newly enacted Maharlika Investment Fund to serve as vehicle for growth,” July 18, 2023. [Source](https://www.dof.gov.ph/newly-enacted-maharlika-investment-fund-to-serve-as-vehicle-for-growth/).

**Locator.** Official announcement of RA No. 11954 and stated goals.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for signing and executive implementation; shared with Congress, which approved the legislation.

**Complicating evidence.** The UP School of Economics, CMFR, TIME, and media reports raised concerns about the fund’s rationale, governance, funding sources, and investor confidence. [UP School of Economics discussion paper](https://econ.upd.edu.ph/discussion-paper/dp-2023-02); [TIME, “The Philippines Just Passed a Bill to Create a Sovereign Wealth Fund”](https://time.com/6241736/philippines-sovereign-wealth-fund).

**Cautions.** Early evidence concerns design and governance, not realized returns. A definitive assessment requires board records, funding flows, procurement, investment decisions, audits, and conflict-of-interest disclosures.

### WEB-5B-06 — The Maharlika Fund was contested in media and legal debate

**Claim and significance.** Independent media-monitoring and policy sources described the Maharlika Fund as controversial and called for greater scrutiny, especially concerning governance, economic rationale, and investor confidence. A petition was also filed asking the Supreme Court to declare the act unconstitutional.

This demonstrates that the policy was contested and subject to public and legal scrutiny rather than silently adopted.

**Sources.**

- CMFR, “Maharlika fund proposal needs more media scrutiny.” [Source](https://cmfr-phil.org/media-ethics-responsibility/journalism-review/cmfr-media-review/maharlika-fund-proposal-needs-more-media-scrutiny)
- Manila Bulletin, “SC asked to declare unconstitutional Maharlika Investment Fund Act.” [Source](https://mb.com.ph/2023/9/18/sc-asked-to-declare-unconstitutional-maharlika-investment-fund-act)
- [TIME, Maharlika Fund analysis](https://time.com/6241736/philippines-sovereign-wealth-fund)

**Locator.** Media-monitoring analysis and September 2023 constitutional challenge.

**Period status.** Target-year evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** High for the administration’s support and signing of the measure; low for the legal outcome unless the Court later ruled on the merits.

**Complicating evidence.** The administration and Finance Department argued that the fund could support infrastructure, investment, jobs, and economic growth. Congressional passage provided formal democratic authorization.

**Cautions.** Criticism of a policy is not proof of corruption or capture. The relevant unresolved questions are governance safeguards, conflicts, funding, and realized performance.

### WEB-5B-07 — Public investment and energy transition were identified as long-term growth priorities

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank identified public investment, transmission capacity, grid flexibility, energy efficiency, renewable energy, water and sanitation, education, health, and digital infrastructure as important to long-term productivity and resilience.

The report also warned that the Philippines remained heavily dependent on fossil fuels and needed a just transition to reduce energy insecurity and future costs.

**Strongest source.** World Bank, *Philippines Economic Update: Securing a Clean Energy Future*, June 2023. [Source](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/c4aa826d0cda3d9b21db1adc4f8e1787-0070062023/original/World-Bank-Philippines-Economic-Update-June-2023.pdf).

**Locator.** pp. 38–64; summary lines 16–21 of the World Bank highlights page. [Highlights](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/philippines/publication/highlights-of-the-philippines-economic-update-june-2023-securing-a-clean-energy-future).

**Period status.** Target-year policy assessment and medium-term outlook.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The administration controlled public investment priorities and energy policy, but infrastructure delivery depended on Congress, agencies, regulators, utilities, and private investors.

**Complicating evidence.** The report recognized that public investment was projected to remain above 5 percent of GDP and that reform could attract investment. It did not establish that projects were completed on time or produced their intended productivity gains.

**Cautions.** Strategy documents and international recommendations should not be treated as evidence of completed implementation.

### WEB-5B-08 — The administration used a temporary rice-price ceiling to address food inflation

**Claim and significance.** Marcos approved Executive Order No. 39 on August 31, 2023, imposing nationwide ceilings of ₱41 per kilogram for regular-milled rice and ₱45 for well-milled rice. The policy responded to rising rice prices, alleged hoarding or manipulation, international supply disruptions, and domestic distribution problems.

**Strongest source.** Presidential Communications Office, “PBBM sets price ceilings on rice nationwide,” August 31, 2023. [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-sets-price-ceilings-on-rice-nationwide/).

**Locator.** Official announcement of EO No. 39 and price levels.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High. Marcos approved the measure and was concurrently serving as agriculture secretary for much of the period.

**Complicating evidence.** The government later lifted the ceiling in October after reporting price stabilization. The Philippine Institute for Development Studies and media analysis warned that price controls could impose losses on retailers and farmers or worsen shortages. [PIDS rice-price analysis](https://pidswebs.pids.gov.ph/CDN/document/1693810949_64f58105963e1.pdf).

**Cautions.** The order’s existence is well documented; its causal effect on prices, farmer income, retailer viability, and food security requires price and market data.

### WEB-5B-09 — The rice-price policy was later lifted, but the government continued to claim a long-term ₱20 target

**Claim and significance.** The administration lifted the rice-price ceiling in October 2023 after reporting that prices had stabilized. Marcos continued to state that ₱20-per-kilogram rice remained attainable, while government releases emphasized supply, distribution, subsidies, and agricultural interventions.

This shows policy correction in response to changing conditions, but also a gap between political promises and demonstrated affordability.

**Sources.**

- PCO, “PBBM announces lifting of price cap on rice.” [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-announces-lifting-of-price-cap-on-rice/)
- PCO, “P20 per kilo of rice still attainable, says PBBM.” [Source](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/p20-per-kilo-of-rice-still-attainable-says-pbbm/)
- PIA, “PH has enough rice supply but government has to address distribution gaps.” [Source](https://mirror.pia.gov.ph/press-releases/2023/09/25/ph-has-enough-rice-supply-but-govt-has-to-address-distribution-gapspbbm)

**Locator.** October lifting announcement and official statements concerning supply, distribution, and the ₱20 target.

**Period status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** High for lifting the price ceiling and maintaining the policy goal; moderate for underlying supply and market outcomes.

**Complicating evidence.** The World Bank emphasized targeted transfers, food stamps, farmer support, and better forecasting rather than price controls alone. The administration’s claim of stabilization should be checked against Philippine Statistics Authority rice-price series.

**Cautions.** Official claims about stabilization and attainability are not independent outcome evidence.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Economic information was relatively accessible through the World Bank, Asian Development Bank, Philippine Statistics Authority, government releases, and international economic reports. The main limitations were attribution and distribution.

Key cautions:

- 2023 growth reflected global and post-pandemic recovery, remittances, services, and domestic consumption as well as government policy.
- Inflation was affected by global commodities, exchange rates, Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, interest rates, weather, and supply chains.
- Current-dollar national accounts should not be interpreted as real welfare growth.
- Government releases establish that policies were announced or implemented, but not that they achieved their intended effects.
- The Maharlika Fund’s ultimate performance cannot be assessed from its 2023 enabling law.
- Rice-price intervention affected consumers, farmers, traders, and retailers differently.
- Poverty and labor outcomes require household survey and productivity data, not GDP alone.
- Infrastructure claims require project-level completion, procurement, audit, and utilization records.

- Commission on Audit reports on infrastructure, agriculture, Maharlika, subsidies, and rice procurement.
- Department of Finance and Bureau of the Treasury 2023 fiscal and debt reports.
- Philippine Statistics Authority household income, poverty, labor, CPI, rice-price, and productivity tables.
- Maharlika Investment Corporation charter, board appointments, source-of-funds records, internal rules, and first audits.
- Detailed National Economic and Development Authority implementation report for the Philippine Development Plan 2023–2028.
- Department of Agriculture distribution, import, buffer-stock, farmer-support, and rice-subsidy records.
- Independent evaluation of the temporary rice-price ceiling’s incidence on consumers, farmers, and retailers.
- Regional and gender-disaggregated poverty and employment outcomes.
- Procurement and completion data for flagship infrastructure projects.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected: 22.
- Accepted evidence sources: 12.
- Corroborating sources: 8.
- Access-blocked or not fully extractable: 7.
- Developed evidence records: 9.
- Strongest positive evidence: continued growth, broadly stable fiscal and debt conditions, investment-oriented reforms, energy-transition planning, and targeted responses to food inflation.
- Strongest adverse evidence: high inflation, disproportionate food-price burdens, low-productivity employment, unresolved distribution problems, and Maharlika governance concerns.
- Main unresolved questions: realized household income and poverty outcomes, execution quality of public investment, economic-professional appointments and autonomy, Maharlika funding and conflicts, and whether rice intervention improved welfare without shifting costs to producers or retailers.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# 6B — Social Well-Being and Human Development

## 1. Orientation

Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr. was president throughout 2023. He controlled the national executive, proposed and implemented the national budget, appointed cabinet officials, and directed agencies including education, health, and social welfare. His influence was constrained by Congress, constitutional commissions, courts, local governments, bureaucratic capacity, fiscal limits, and inherited conditions from the pandemic and prior administrations.

The administration’s stated social agenda emphasized food security, education, health care, social protection, and “inclusive and sustainable” growth. The 2023 budget allocated substantial resources to education, health, social protection, and vulnerable groups. However, many outcome indicators available for 2023 measure conditions inherited from earlier years or reflect policies whose effects would emerge later. The evidence supports a record of significant welfare-oriented budgeting and targeted assistance, alongside persistent educational, regional, sanitation, poverty, and service-quality gaps.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-6B-01 | 2023 social-policy budget | Education, health, social protection, and food security were formal budget priorities. | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-02 | Social-protection allocations | The proposed budget included ₱552.4 billion for social protection, including ₱115.6 billion for 4Ps and ₱25.3 billion for indigent senior citizens. | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.7 |
| WEB-6B-03 | Health allocations | The budget provided ₱296.3–299 billion for health, including insurance, facilities, medicines, workforce, and indigent-patient assistance. | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.4, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-04 | Education allocations | ₱852.8 billion was allocated to education, including teachers, classrooms, learning materials, technology, scholarships, and subsidies. | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.4, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-05 | Poverty and macroeconomic conditions | Growth remained positive, but inflation, borrowing costs, weak trade, and low-productivity employment constrained welfare gains. | 6B.6, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-06 | Water and sanitation access | National access was high but sharply unequal by region, especially for BARMM and rural populations. | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-07 | Food-price intervention | Marcos imposed and later lifted a nationwide rice price ceiling during a food-price shock. | 6B.1, 6B.6, 6B.7 |
| WEB-6B-08 | Education and life-chance baseline | Learning poverty and education quality remained major inherited constraints; 2023 spending does not establish immediate learning improvement. | 6B.5, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-09 | Measurement and accountability | National statistical agencies and international organizations supplied usable data, but outcome attribution and program-effectiveness evidence remained incomplete. | 6B.5, 6B.10 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-6B-01 — Social welfare was an explicit national budget priority

The 2023 proposed national budget framed the government’s agenda around “inclusive and sustainable” growth and identified food security, health care, social protection, education, and fiscal management as core priorities. This is evidence of formal welfare intent, though not proof of successful implementation.

- **Source:** *2023 People’s Proposed Budget*, Department of Budget and Management, 2022.
- **Locator:** pp. 3 and 9; lines describing the eight-point socioeconomic agenda and sector allocations.
- **Period fit:** 2023 policy and budget.
- **Attribution:** High for formal executive budgeting; moderate for actual delivery because the document is a proposal and government presentation.
- **Cautions:** It reports planned allocations and official rationale, not independent expenditure quality or outcomes.
- **Corroboration:** NEDA’s *Philippine Development Report 2023* and the World Bank’s 2023 Philippines Economic Update describe development, poverty, employment, and service-delivery priorities.

### WEB-6B-02 — Social-protection spending was large and targeted

The 2023 budget proposed ₱552.4 billion for social protection, including ₱115.6 billion for the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program, ₱75.7 billion for survivors, internally displaced persons, gender-and-development programs, and disaster relief, and ₱25.5 billion for senior citizens. The budget described 4Ps as providing education, health, and rice subsidies to 4.4 million households and allocated ₱25.3 billion for a social pension for 4.1 million indigent senior citizens.

- **Source:** *2023 People’s Proposed Budget*, Department of Budget and Management, 2022.
- **Locator:** pp. 9 and 24; lines 358–364 and 1007–1019.
- **Period fit:** 2023 planned spending.
- **Attribution:** High for Marcos administration priorities and the executive budget proposal; lower for realized benefit incidence.
- **Cautions:** The document does not establish whether all intended beneficiaries received benefits promptly or equitably.
- **Contrary evidence/complication:** The scale of the allocation indicates welfare priority, but poverty and inequality outcomes remained dependent on inflation, local delivery, targeting accuracy, and administrative capacity.
- **Corroboration:** World Bank discussion of 4Ps and targeted social protection; Commission on Audit materials on Pantawid implementation and follow-up performance auditing.

### WEB-6B-03 — Health received substantial planned resources, including support for disadvantaged patients

The 2023 budget proposed approximately ₱296.3–299 billion for health. It included ₱63.7 billion for health-facility operations, ₱23 billion for facility enhancement, ₱16.9 billion for a national health workforce support system, ₱29.1 billion for medicines and vaccines, ₱100.2 billion for national health insurance, and ₱22.4 billion for medical assistance to indigent and financially incapacitated patients.

- **Source:** *2023 People’s Proposed Budget*, Department of Budget and Management, 2022.
- **Locator:** pp. 9 and 26–29; lines 1068–1096 and 1134–1193.
- **Period fit:** 2023 planned spending.
- **Attribution:** High for formal budget choices; moderate for implementation.
- **Cautions:** Budget documents may count appropriations or intended beneficiaries rather than actual service utilization, quality, or geographic equity.
- **Contrary evidence/complication:** The same budget identified staffing, remote-area deployment, hospital capacity, medicines, and public-health surveillance as continuing needs, indicating that formal allocation did not eliminate service deficits.
- **Corroboration:** WHO Philippines materials, World Bank country analysis, and Philippine Development Report 2023.

### WEB-6B-04 — Education was the largest identified sectoral priority

The 2023 budget allocated ₱852.8 billion to education: ₱710.7 billion for basic education, ₱128.5 billion for higher education, and ₱13.7 billion for technical-vocational education. It also proposed 10,000 additional teaching positions, flexible-learning materials for 24.5 million students, construction or rehabilitation of schools and classrooms, electricity for 163 sites, computerization packages, subsidies for 2.6 million private-school students, and scholarships and training programs.

- **Source:** *2023 People’s Proposed Budget*, Department of Budget and Management, 2022.
- **Locator:** pp. 30–31; lines 1221–1301.
- **Period fit:** 2023 budget.
- **Attribution:** High for the administration’s stated priority and resource proposal; moderate for delivery.
- **Cautions:** These are planned inputs, not evidence that classrooms, teachers, devices, or learning materials were delivered on schedule or improved learning.
- **Contrary evidence:** The World Bank’s Learning Poverty Brief identifies severe pre-existing learning challenges. Enrollment levels therefore cannot be treated as equivalent to learning quality.
- **Corroboration:** World Bank Learning Poverty Brief; PSA education indicators; NEDA development reporting.

### WEB-6B-05 — Growth continued, but inflation and low-quality employment constrained welfare gains

The World Bank projected Philippine growth at approximately 5.6 percent in 2023. It attributed resilience to household consumption, remittances, services, tourism, and IT-BPO activity, but warned that high inflation, elevated borrowing costs, geopolitical uncertainty, and weak external demand constrained households and private investment. The World Bank also emphasized that recovery could coexist with labor-market movement toward lower-productivity employment and recommended targeted social protection for poor and vulnerable households.

- **Source:** *Implementing Reforms to Boost Private Investment Key to Sustained Job Creation and Poverty Reduction in the Philippines*, World Bank, 5 December 2023.
- **Locator:** Executive summary and sections on growth, inflation, poverty, social protection, and structural reforms.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** Low to moderate for the macroeconomic outcome; higher for the administration’s policy environment and crisis-response choices. Growth and inflation were also shaped by global conditions, monetary policy, commodity prices, and private-sector behavior.
- **Cautions:** GDP growth is not a direct measure of household welfare or distribution.
- **Corroboration:** Asian Development Bank, *Asian Development Outlook September 2023*; Philippine Statistics Authority; World Bank June 2023 Economic Update.

### WEB-6B-06 — Water access was widespread but regionally unequal

The Philippine Statistics Authority’s 2022 Annual Poverty Indicators Survey reported that 97.6 percent of families had an improved drinking-water source and 96.3 percent had a basic drinking-water service level. However, basic access was 87.8 percent in BARMM, compared with 99.3 percent in the National Capital Region and Central Luzon. Basic sanitation reached 84.0 percent nationally but only 51.6 percent in BARMM.

- **Source:** *Majority of Families Have Access to Improved Source of Drinking Water*, Philippine Statistics Authority, 15 July 2021/updated release concerning the 2022 APIS.
- **Locator:** Sections “Drinking Water” and “Sanitation.”
- **Period fit:** Inherited/baseline evidence available during the 2023 period, not a 2023 outcome.
- **Attribution:** Low for Marcos personally; these are household conditions accumulated over prior administrations and influenced by local governments and infrastructure providers.
- **Cautions:** The survey measures access and service level, not reliability, affordability, contamination, or actual health effects.
- **Contrary evidence/complication:** National averages conceal severe regional disparities, particularly in BARMM and other poorer regions.
- **Corroboration:** UNICEF/WHO Joint Monitoring Programme data, UN country data, and Philippine Development Report materials.

### WEB-6B-07 — Rice-price intervention addressed an immediate food-affordability shock but was temporary

Marcos issued Executive Order No. 39 in September 2023 imposing nationwide maximum prices of ₱41 per kilogram for regular-milled rice and ₱45 for well-milled rice. The measure was later lifted as supply and market conditions changed. The intervention shows direct executive action in response to food-price pressure, but its temporary nature and the administration’s simultaneous reliance on supply, import, subsidy, and enforcement measures limit conclusions about durable food security.

- **Source:** *PBBM Sets Price Ceilings on Rice Nationwide*, Presidential Communications Office, 2023.
- **Locator:** Executive Order No. 39 and subsequent official announcements lifting the price cap.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** High for the executive decision; moderate for effects on consumers and producers.
- **Cautions:** Government releases emphasize stabilization and intent; independent evidence is needed to assess compliance, shortages, producer losses, and distributional effects.
- **Contrary evidence/complication:** Rice-price controls may protect consumers temporarily while creating enforcement, supply, or producer-incentive problems if maintained without addressing structural supply and distribution constraints.
- **Corroboration:** World Bank food-security analysis, PSA price data, and independent Philippine economic reporting.

### WEB-6B-08 — Education spending confronted a severe inherited learning problem

The World Bank’s Philippines Learning Poverty Brief identified learning poverty as a major constraint: many children were not achieving minimum reading proficiency by the end of primary school, even where enrollment was comparatively high. This means the government’s large 2023 education allocation addressed an important inherited deficit, but the available evidence does not establish that the Marcos administration had already produced measurable improvement in learning outcomes by the end of 2023.

- **Source:** *Philippines – Learning Poverty Brief – 2022*, World Bank, 2022.
- **Locator:** Country learning-poverty indicator and education-system sections.
- **Period fit:** Inherited baseline used to assess 2023 policy.
- **Attribution:** Low for the underlying condition; moderate for assessing whether 2023 education policy was appropriately directed.
- **Cautions:** The brief is not a 2023 outcome evaluation and should not be used to attribute the entire learning deficit to Marcos.
- **Corroboration:** UNICEF education materials, PSA education statistics, World Bank Philippines Economic Updates, and NEDA reporting.

### WEB-6B-09 — Welfare measurement was relatively strong, but attribution and implementation evidence remained incomplete

The Philippines had usable public data from PSA, World Bank, ADB, UN agencies, budget documents, and audit institutions. These sources permit assessment of poverty, water, sanitation, enrollment, spending, inflation, and macroeconomic conditions. However, several important questions remain difficult: actual agency execution versus proposed budgets; benefit leakage and exclusion; service quality; subnational variation; and whether 2023 interventions caused measurable changes rather than merely continuing inherited programs.

- **Sources:** PSA APIS release; DBM 2023 budget; World Bank and ADB country updates; COA audit catalogue.
- **Period fit:** Mixed: 2023 policy/outcome data and inherited baselines.
- **Attribution:** Usually moderate or low for broad national outcomes; high only for identifiable executive acts and budget decisions.
- **Cautions:** Official performance documents may overstate accomplishment; international datasets may lag; one-year changes cannot establish durable life-chance improvement.
- **Corroboration:** Independent development assessments and audit records.

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **6B.1 — Welfare as a governing purpose:** Supported by the 2023 budget and formal priorities. The record shows substantial planned welfare spending, but implementation and distributive results require further verification.
- **6B.2 — Access to essential services:** Mixed. Budgeted support for health, education, water, and social protection was substantial; access remained uneven and service quality is insufficiently documented.
- **6B.3 — Priority for vulnerable groups:** Supported in formal design through 4Ps, senior pensions, indigent-patient assistance, disaster relief, and IDP programs. Actual inclusion and regional incidence remain gaps.
- **6B.4 — Professional service delivery:** Partially documented. The budget funded health workers, teachers, facilities, and delivery systems, but evidence on staffing quality, procurement, execution, and administrative professionalism is incomplete.
- **6B.5 — Measurement and correction:** Partially supported. PSA, World Bank, ADB, and audit systems provided measurement. Evidence that Marcos systematically corrected failed programs based on independent evaluation is limited.
- **6B.6 — Protection during crises:** Mixed. Rice controls and targeted transfers addressed food and inflation pressures, but the durability, targeting, and effectiveness of crisis responses require further study.
- **6B.7 — No political allocation of welfare:** No strong evidence in the inspected sources establishes systematic political conditioning of the listed national programs. This remains an important research gap.
- **6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity:** Formal programs targeted indigent households, older people, persons with disabilities, students, and remote communities. Regional disparities in water, sanitation, education, and health indicate incomplete equal access.
- **6B.9 — Durable social institutions:** Existing institutions such as 4Ps, PhilHealth, DepEd, DOH, PSA, and COA continued to operate. The 2023 record does not yet demonstrate that Marcos created durable improvements beyond those inherited institutions.
- **6B.10 — Lasting life chances:** Not determinable from 2023 evidence alone. Growth and large social allocations were favorable conditions, but learning, poverty, sanitation, health quality, and regional-equity outcomes require longer time series.

## 5. Information environment and evidence limitations

The evidence environment was comparatively open for budgets, national statistics, international development assessments, and formal policy documents. PSA, DBM, COA, World Bank, ADB, UNICEF, WHO, and UN sources provide useful independent or semi-independent material.

The main limitations are:

- budget documents describe appropriations and intended beneficiaries, not necessarily execution;
- many social indicators available in 2023 measured 2022 or earlier conditions;
- national averages conceal major regional differences, especially in BARMM and poorer rural regions;
- one year is too short to assess durable changes in learning, health, poverty, or life expectancy;
- government accomplishment reports are useful for identifying actions but may emphasize achievements;
- program effects are difficult to attribute to Marcos because Congress, agencies, local governments, donors, and prior administrations shared responsibility;
- inflation, commodity prices, remittances, global growth, and post-pandemic recovery affected outcomes independently of presidential policy.
