--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# 7B — Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty

## 1. Orientation

Ferdinand Marcos Jr. held the Philippine presidency throughout 2023. His formal authority included appointments, budget proposals, executive administration, and supervision of national agencies. His practical authority was constrained by Congress, courts, constitutional commissions, the Commission on Audit, the civil service, coalition politics, and investigative media.

The 2023 evidence does not establish a final judicial finding that Marcos personally stole public funds or received an illegal benefit during the year. It does, however, document several integrity concerns: appointments of campaign donors, concentration of family and political networks around senior offices, rapid enactment of the Maharlika Investment Fund despite expert objections, substantial confidential-fund spending with limited public visibility, and official explanations that require comparison with documentary records. These are evidence of exposure to conflicts, patronage, and weak transparency—not automatic proof of personal criminal wrongdoing.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-7B-01 | Campaign donors appointed to office | PCIJ identified at least six Marcos campaign donors appointed to government posts. | 7B.3, 7B.5, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-02 | Family and political-network appointments | Key appointments involved close political, family, business, or campaign relationships. | 7B.5, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-03 | Maharlika safeguards | The enacted MIF law included conflict-of-interest and governance restrictions. | 7B.3, 7B.6, 7B.7 |
| WEB-7B-04 | Maharlika process and transparency concerns | Independent economists and media monitors criticized the speed, governance, funding, and transparency of the fund. | 7B.2, 7B.3, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-05 | Confidential-fund opacity | The Office of the President spent more than ₱2.2 billion in confidential funds in 2023, according to reporting on COA records. | 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.10 |
| WEB-7B-06 | Official response to confidential-fund concerns | The administration said confidential and intelligence funds were covered by rules and properly accounted for. | 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.7 |
| WEB-7B-07 | Sugar-importation explanation | Marcos described the 2023 sugar-importation controversy as a procedural mistake; the statement requires comparison with administrative records and accountability findings. | 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.8 |
| WEB-7B-08 | Institutional accountability | COA, Congress, courts, investigative journalists, and civil-society experts remained able to scrutinize presidential policies, though access and transparency were uneven. | 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.10 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-7B-01 — Campaign donors received government appointments

The Philippine Center for Investigative Journalism identified at least six Marcos campaign donors who received government appointments. The reported appointees included officials in Malacañang, agriculture, the Judicial and Bar Council, the National Housing Authority, and the Philippine Charity Sweepstakes Office. PCIJ reported that Francisco Tiu Laurel Jr., later appointed agriculture secretary, had donated ₱30 million to Marcos’s political party; Melquiades Robles donated ₱30 million directly to the campaign and became PCSO chair; and other donors received senior executive, housing, or judicial-selection roles.

- **Source:** “At least 6 Marcos donors in 2022 polls given gov’t posts,” Philippine Center for Investigative Journalism, 3 November 2023.
- **Locator:** The article’s opening paragraphs and sections on Robles, Lagdameo, Guevara, Firme, and Ang Tai.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns appointments held during the Marcos presidency; some appointments began in 2022.
- **Attribution:** High: appointments were presidential or executive appointments, and campaign donations were drawn from Comelec declarations and public records.
- **Significance:** The pattern creates a documented conflict-of-interest risk and raises questions about whether access, loyalty, or campaign support influenced appointments.
- **Cautions:** PCIJ did not establish that any appointment was illegal or that a donor received a contract or improper financial benefit. Philippine law did not categorically prohibit appointing campaign contributors.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some appointees had relevant qualifications or prior public-service experience. The evidence supports scrutiny, not a conclusion of bribery or personal enrichment.
- **Independent corroboration:** Presidential appointment records, Comelec campaign-finance declarations, and official biographies should be checked for each appointee.

### WEB-7B-02 — Appointments combined political, family, and business-network ties

The PCIJ investigation reported that several senior appointments connected the administration to established political or business networks. Anton Ernesto Lagdameo Jr., appointed special assistant to the president, was a party treasurer and donor and was also described as the grandson of a business associate of Ferdinand Marcos Sr. Joeben Ang Tai, appointed National Housing Authority general manager, was a campaign donor with a background in real estate. Nesauro Firme, a campaign contributor and legal figure, became the academe representative on the Judicial and Bar Council, which nominates judicial candidates.

- **Source:** “At least 6 Marcos donors in 2022 polls given gov’t posts,” Philippine Center for Investigative Journalism, 3 November 2023.
- **Locator:** Lines concerning Lagdameo, Firme, and Ang Tai.
- **Period fit:** 2023 ruler-period evidence, with some appointments dating from 2022.
- **Attribution:** High for the fact of appointments; moderate for any inference about motive or influence.
- **Significance:** These relationships are relevant to nepotism, clientelism, and institutional independence, particularly where appointees occupied roles affecting appointments, housing, or public funds.
- **Cautions:** Kinship or prior association does not by itself prove favoritism. The source did not demonstrate that any appointee acted unlawfully.
- **Contrary evidence:** Firme had academic and government-consulting experience; Guevara had prior SEC enforcement experience. Qualifications and network ties must be assessed together.

### WEB-7B-03 — The Maharlika law created formal integrity safeguards

Republic Act No. 11954 and its implementing rules established the Maharlika Investment Fund and included conflict-of-interest restrictions. The implementing rules prohibited regular and independent directors from owning, managing, or working for private enterprises with material transactions or conflicting interests involving the Maharlika Investment Corporation, subject to legal exceptions. The rules also prohibited certain recommendations and imposed a post-employment restriction.

- **Source:** “Revised IRR of the Maharlika Investment Fund Act of 2023,” Lawphil, 2023.
- **Locator:** Sections 12–13 and the provisions on director conflicts and post-employment restrictions.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023 legislation and implementation.
- **Attribution:** High for the legal framework: Marcos signed the enabling law and the executive branch implemented it.
- **Significance:** The law provides evidence that the administration formally recognized conflict-of-interest and accountability risks.
- **Cautions:** Formal safeguards do not establish independent enforcement. The effectiveness of the rules depends on appointments, disclosure, audit, investment transparency, and sanctions.
- **Contrary evidence:** The fund was criticized for governance ambiguity and political-risk exposure before and after enactment.

### WEB-7B-04 — Maharlika was enacted rapidly despite sustained concerns about governance and transparency

The Center for Media Freedom and Responsibility reported that the Maharlika proposal moved through Congress rapidly after Marcos certified it as urgent. Economists and analysts criticized the proposed governance structure, use of government-bank capital, safeguards, and transparency. CMFR also reported that the fund’s creation involved the president’s cousin, House Speaker Martin Romualdez, and his son, Representative Sandro Marcos, as prominent legislative sponsors or proponents.

A separate CMFR review in October 2023 reported that foreign investors remained concerned about the fund’s transparency, institutional structure, and investor safeguards.

- **Sources:** “Maharlika fund proposal needs more media scrutiny,” Center for Media Freedom and Responsibility, 2023; “Inquirer report reveals why foreign investors are wary of Maharlika fund,” CMFR, 6 October 2023.
- **Locators:** First article, sections on legislative speed, expert objections, and Marcos’s role; second article, paragraphs 32–49.
- **Period fit:** 2023 policy and implementation evidence; some concerns originated in late 2022.
- **Attribution:** High for Marcos’s certification and public sponsorship; moderate for legislative conduct because Congress independently enacted the law.
- **Significance:** The episode tests whether the president accepted independent scrutiny and corrected weaknesses before using public financial institutions.
- **Cautions:** CMFR is a media-monitoring organization and summarizes other reporting; it is not a judicial or audit finding.
- **Contrary evidence:** The enacted law and IRR added safeguards, excluded several pension and social-insurance funds from mandatory capitalization, and created formal governance provisions. Supporters argued that the fund could finance infrastructure and development.

### WEB-7B-05 — Confidential-fund spending limited public transparency

Reporting based on Commission on Audit records stated that the Office of the President spent more than ₱2.2 billion in confidential funds in 2023. Confidential expenditures may legitimately require secrecy, but they remain subject to audit and documentation rules. The large amount and limited public detail made it difficult for journalists, legislators, and citizens to independently assess the purpose and legality of expenditures.

- **Source:** “COA: Marcos’ office spent over P2B in confidential funds in 2023,” Philippine Daily Inquirer, 2024, reporting on COA records.
- **Locator:** Reported 2023 expenditure figure and discussion of COA reporting.
- **Period fit:** 2023 expenditure; source is a later report based on annual audit records.
- **Attribution:** High for expenditure by the Office of the President; lower for personal responsibility for each disbursement.
- **Significance:** This is relevant to concealment, auditability, and the ruler’s willingness to accept independent accountability.
- **Cautions:** The underlying Inquirer page was inaccessible during inspection; the claim should be rechecked directly against the relevant COA annual audit report before final publication.
- **Contrary evidence:** The administration stated that confidential and intelligence funds were governed by a 2015 joint circular and were properly accounted for. Confidentiality can be lawful if documentation is supplied to authorized auditors.

### WEB-7B-06 — The administration defended confidential and intelligence funds as regulated and accountable

The Presidential Communications Office reported the Department of Budget and Management’s position that confidential and intelligence funds were covered by an existing joint circular and that administrative and disbursing officers remained accountable. The statement also gave the national share of confidential and intelligence funds in the 2023 budget as approximately 0.190 percent.

- **Source:** “DBM: Intelligence funds are covered by circular and properly accounted for,” Presidential Communications Office, 2023.
- **Locator:** DBM briefing and statements on the 2015 joint circular and accountability of finance, administrative, and disbursing officers.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns the 2023 controversy.
- **Attribution:** High for the administration’s official position; low as independent evidence that every expenditure was proper.
- **Significance:** It provides the ruler’s defense and should be compared with COA findings, not treated as proof of compliance.
- **Cautions:** The source is government communication and is inherently self-serving.
- **Contrary evidence:** Later COA-related reporting questioned documentation and transparency concerning confidential-fund use by executive offices.

### WEB-7B-07 — Marcos characterized the sugar-importation controversy as a procedural mistake

During the 2023 sugar-importation controversy, Marcos reportedly described the problem as a “procedural mistake.” The statement establishes the administration’s explanation, but it does not by itself resolve whether officials exceeded authority, whether importation was properly authorized, or whether accountability followed.

- **Source:** “Marcos asserts sugar importation mess was only a ‘procedural mistake’,” Manila Bulletin, 5 January 2023.
- **Locator:** Headline and interview account concerning Marcos’s characterization of the controversy.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** High for the reported presidential statement.
- **Significance:** This is a testable example for truthfulness, correction, and good-faith explanation during a politically costly administrative failure.
- **Cautions:** The source records a characterization, not an adjudication of the underlying legal facts.
- **Contrary evidence:** The Sugar Regulatory Administration’s records, congressional inquiries, administrative findings, and subsequent personnel decisions should establish whether the issue was merely procedural or involved unauthorized action.

### WEB-7B-08 — Independent accountability mechanisms continued to operate, but access was uneven

The 2023 record shows that COA, Congress, courts, investigative media, civil-society economists, and media-monitoring organizations continued to scrutinize presidential initiatives. The Maharlika Fund was challenged publicly by economists and legislators; its legality and safeguards were debated; and government spending remained subject to audit rules. However, confidential funds, family-linked appointments, and limited access to underlying financial information restricted the public’s ability to verify official claims.

- **Sources:** Maharlika IRR, Lawphil; CMFR Maharlika reviews; PCIJ donor-appointment investigation; COA-related reporting.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns the 2023 ruler-period.
- **Attribution:** Moderate to high for the preservation of formal scrutiny; moderate for the president’s willingness to accept it.
- **Cautions:** Continued scrutiny does not establish that findings were fully remedied or that institutions were fully independent.
- **Contrary evidence:** The speed of Maharlika’s enactment and the opacity of confidential-fund spending suggest limits on effective public accountability.

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **7B.1 — Truthfulness:** Mixed and incomplete. The record contains verifiable official statements, including the sugar-importation explanation and confidential-fund defense, but the underlying factual disputes require additional primary records. No comprehensive 2023 truthfulness finding is justified.
- **7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors:** Partially documented. The administration acknowledged or characterized controversies, but evidence of full correction, public retraction, or remedial accountability is limited.
- **7B.3 — Conflicts of interest:** Material risk documented. Campaign-donor appointments and Maharlika governance concerns warrant scrutiny; formal MIF conflict rules provide some contrary evidence.
- **7B.4 — Personal profit from office:** No inspected source established that Marcos personally or his immediate family illegally profited from public office during 2023. Historical allegations and inherited family-wealth disputes should be kept separate from target-year findings.
- **7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments:** Appointment networks involving donors, relatives, political allies, and business figures are documented. Whether appointments were primarily based on loyalty rather than competence remains mixed and case-specific.
- **7B.6 — Independent investigation:** Formal oversight remained possible through COA, Congress, courts, and investigative journalism. Access to confidential spending and politically connected appointments remained limited.
- **7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment:** No clear target-year judicial finding of Marcos personally obstructing an investigation was located. Confidential-fund opacity and rapid legislative processing are accountability concerns, not proof of obstruction.
- **7B.8 — Promises and good faith:** The Maharlika process and sugar-importation explanation present disputed cases requiring comparison of statements, legislative records, and implementation. The evidence is insufficient for a general conclusion.
- **7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism:** Strongest adverse evidence concerns donor-linked appointments and family-political networks. No inspected source proved a specific corrupt contract or personal financial exchange.
- **7B.10 — Ethical example and public trust:** The record is mixed. Formal safeguards and continued oversight coexist with patronage risks, opacity, and controversial use of public resources.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Integrity research was possible because Comelec declarations, appointment records, legislative documents, Lawphil, COA materials, investigative journalism, and civil-society analysis were publicly accessible. However:

- allegations and documented conflicts are often reported before courts or auditors reach final findings;
- government statements are strong evidence that a claim was made, but weak evidence that it was true;
- confidential-fund documentation is not fully public;
- donor-appointment reporting identifies relationships but cannot alone prove quid pro quo;
- family and historical wealth allegations can contaminate analysis of 2023 conduct unless clearly separated;
- some important reporting was inaccessible or available only through secondary summaries;
- the political polarization surrounding the Marcos family makes independent corroboration especially important.

- Comelec campaign-finance filings for every donor named by PCIJ.
- Appointment papers, qualification records, and ethics disclosures for donor-linked appointees.
- Sugar Regulatory Administration resolutions and congressional records concerning the sugar-importation controversy.
- Supreme Court filings and decisions concerning the Maharlika Investment Fund.

- Direct inspection of the government’s fact-check archive and presidential statements on disputed historical or contemporary claims.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence

## 1. Orientation

Ferdinand Marcos Jr. held the Philippine presidency throughout 2023. He controlled the national executive, proposed the national budget, issued executive orders, appointed cabinet officials, and directed foreign, security, economic, agricultural, and social policy. His authority was constrained by Congress, courts, constitutional commissions, local governments, bureaucratic capacity, coalition politics, and external economic and security conditions.

The administration’s principal stated program combined the 8-Point Socioeconomic Agenda, the Philippine Development Plan 2023–2028, infrastructure and investment promotion, food security, social protection, digital government, and a more active security and foreign-policy posture. In 2023, the government converted several priorities into laws, budgets, executive actions, and international arrangements. Implementation was uneven: some reforms became operational policy, while other initiatives—especially the Maharlika Investment Fund and long-term infrastructure or digitalization programs—were still at the institution-building stage. Macroeconomic growth remained positive, but inflation, food prices, weak external demand, and climate shocks complicated delivery.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-8B-01 | Program clarity | The administration’s program was expressed through the 8-Point Socioeconomic Agenda, PDP 2023–2028, budget documents, and security policy. | 8B.1, 8B.2 |
| WEB-8B-02 | Budget conversion | Education, health, social protection, food security, infrastructure, and digitalization received formal budget and policy treatment. | 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.6 |
| WEB-8B-03 | Maharlika implementation | Marcos signed the MIF law; its governing corporation and safeguards were established, but full operation was deferred. | 8B.2, 8B.6, 8B.10 |
| WEB-8B-04 | Investment and regulatory reforms | Public-services, foreign-investment, retail-trade, renewable-energy, PPP, digitalization, and financial-inclusion reforms were identified by the World Bank as enacted or underway. | 8B.2, 8B.6, 8B.7 |
| WEB-8B-05 | Growth and constraints | Growth was estimated at 5.6% in 2023, but inflation, borrowing costs, trade weakness, geopolitical uncertainty, and climate risks constrained implementation. | 8B.7, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-06 | Food-price management | The administration imposed and later lifted a nationwide rice-price ceiling during a food-price crisis. | 8B.5, 8B.8, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-07 | Foreign and security repositioning | Marcos expanded defense and diplomatic cooperation with the United States and Japan while managing tensions with China. | 8B.2, 8B.5, 8B.9 |
| WEB-8B-08 | Program reporting and attribution | Official accomplishment reports document activity but require independent verification of completion, quality, and causal effects. | 8B.5, 8B.7, 8B.8, 8B.10 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-8B-01 — The administration had a sufficiently identifiable governing program

The Marcos administration’s stated program was organized around the 8-Point Socioeconomic Agenda, the Philippine Development Plan 2023–2028, fiscal consolidation, food security, infrastructure, health, education, social protection, digital government, and investment promotion. The 2023 budget explicitly presented these areas as administration priorities.

- **Sources:** [2023 People’s Proposed Budget](https://www.dbm.gov.ph/images/pdffiles/2023-Peoples-Proposed-Budget.pdf), Department of Budget and Management; [Philippine Development Report 2023](https://pdp.depdev.gov.ph/pdr-2023), NEDA; [2023 State of the Nation Address](https://stateofthenation.gov.ph/sona/2023), Office of the President.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023 program-setting.
- **Attribution:** High for formal executive goals and budget priorities.
- **Significance:** Clear objectives make implementation and results testable.
- **Cautions:** Official program statements may contain broad aspirations; they do not establish priority in actual spending or successful delivery.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some objectives, including the Maharlika Fund and large infrastructure initiatives, remained at the institutional or planning stage by the end of 2023.

### WEB-8B-02 — Major goals were translated into a formal national budget

The 2023 proposed budget allocated ₱852.8 billion to education, approximately ₱299 billion to health, and ₱552.4 billion to social protection. It also identified food security, infrastructure, disaster resilience, labor, science and technology, and digitalization as spending priorities. The budget specified intended outputs such as teacher positions, classrooms, school rehabilitation, health-workforce deployment, medicines, social transfers, and assistance for vulnerable groups.

- **Source:** [2023 People’s Proposed Budget](https://www.dbm.gov.ph/images/pdffiles/2023-Peoples-Proposed-Budget.pdf), Department of Budget and Management, 2022.
- **Locator:** Sectoral allocations and “Spending Priorities” sections, especially education, health, and social protection.
- **Period fit:** 2023 planned resources.
- **Attribution:** High for the executive proposal; moderate for enacted and executed spending.
- **Significance:** Shows conversion of stated goals into resource commitments.
- **Cautions:** A proposed budget does not establish actual obligation, disbursement, procurement quality, or service outcomes.
- **Corroboration:** Philippine Development Report 2023, PSA indicators, World Bank and ADB economic updates.

### WEB-8B-03 — Marcos converted the Maharlika initiative into law, but durable operation was deferred

Marcos signed Republic Act No. 11954 on 18 July 2023, creating the Philippines’ first sovereign wealth fund. The Department of Finance described the fund as an instrument to finance infrastructure, attract investment, and support the administration’s Medium-Term Fiscal Framework, 8-Point Socioeconomic Agenda, and Philippine Development Plan. The law created the Maharlika Investment Corporation, audit and risk committees, independent directors, congressional oversight, and Commission on Audit examination. The Department of Finance stated that the corporation was expected to become fully operational by the end of 2024.

- **Source:** [Newly enacted Maharlika Investment Fund to serve as vehicle for growth](https://www.dof.gov.ph/newly-enacted-maharlika-investment-fund-to-serve-as-vehicle-for-growth/), Department of Finance, 18 July 2023.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** High for presidential signature and executive implementation.
- **Significance:** Demonstrates legislative and institutional conversion of a flagship goal.
- **Cautions:** The fund had not yet demonstrated investment returns, infrastructure delivery, or durable development effects during 2023.
- **Contrary evidence:** Economists and media monitors questioned the fund’s financing, safeguards, transparency, and governance; these concerns are recorded separately in the integrity and economic chapters.
- **Corroboration:** [Maharlika implementing rules](https://www.pna.gov.ph/articles/1208751); [Maharlika IRR text](https://lawphil.net/statutes/repacts/ra2023/irr_11954b_2023.html).

### WEB-8B-04 — The administration enacted or pursued a broad investment and regulatory-reform agenda

The World Bank reported that the Philippines had enacted reforms involving the Public Services Act, Retail Trade Liberalization Act, Foreign Investment Act, Renewable Energy Act implementing rules, and a new Public-Private Partnership Code. It also identified reforms intended to improve competition in digital services, connectivity, financial inclusion, and government service delivery through digitalization.

- **Source:** [Implementing Reforms to Boost Private Investment Key to Sustained Job Creation and Poverty Reduction in the Philippines](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph), World Bank, 5 December 2023.
- **Locator:** Sections on enacted investment, PPP, digital, and financial-inclusion reforms.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns the policy environment during 2023, although several laws predated the year.
- **Attribution:** Moderate to high. Marcos’s administration implemented and promoted the reform agenda, but Congress and earlier administrations contributed to the underlying legislation.
- **Significance:** Provides stronger evidence of concrete policy machinery than general presidential rhetoric.
- **Cautions:** The World Bank described reforms as important for future investment and job creation; it did not establish that they had already produced large 2023 outcomes.
- **Contrary evidence:** Implementation capacity, regulatory consistency, and investor confidence remained unresolved.

### WEB-8B-05 — Growth continued, but external and domestic constraints limited effectiveness

The World Bank estimated Philippine growth at 5.6% in 2023, driven by household consumption, remittances, services, tourism, and IT-BPO activity. It simultaneously identified high inflation, elevated borrowing costs, sluggish international trade, geopolitical uncertainty, climate shocks, and El Niño as constraints. It recommended full implementation of reforms, targeted social protection, improved food-supply planning, fiscal consolidation, and stronger water, sanitation, education, and health systems.

- **Source:** [World Bank Philippines Economic Update, December 2023](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/05/implementing-reforms-to-boost-private-investment-key-to-sustained-job-creation-and-poverty-reduction-in-ph), World Bank, 5 December 2023.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** Moderate for policy choices; low to moderate for aggregate growth because external conditions and private-sector activity were important.
- **Significance:** Separates observed economic performance from presidential causation.
- **Cautions:** GDP growth is not equivalent to successful implementation of the administration’s full program.
- **Contrary evidence:** The same report’s emphasis on reforms still requiring full implementation limits any claim of completed success.

### WEB-8B-06 — Rice-price intervention showed crisis responsiveness but not durable resolution

Marcos imposed nationwide rice-price ceilings in 2023 and later lifted them. The intervention directly addressed food affordability and demonstrated rapid executive action. However, its temporary nature meant that it did not by itself resolve supply, importation, distribution, agricultural productivity, or market-enforcement problems.

- **Source:** [PBBM Sets Price Ceilings on Rice Nationwide](https://pco.gov.ph/news_releases/pbbm-sets-price-ceilings-on-rice-nationwide/), Presidential Communications Office, 2023.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023.
- **Attribution:** High for the executive decision.
- **Significance:** Tests crisis management, correction, and ability to translate a political priority into immediate action.
- **Cautions:** Official reporting emphasizes stabilization; independent evidence is needed to evaluate compliance, producer effects, shortages, and distribution.
- **Contrary evidence:** The later lifting of the cap and continued food-price concerns indicate a temporary intervention rather than a completed structural solution.
- **Corroboration:** World Bank food-security analysis, PSA price data, and independent economic reporting.

### WEB-8B-07 — Foreign-policy goals were converted into concrete defense and diplomatic arrangements

AMTI reported that Marcos shifted Philippine foreign policy toward deeper cooperation with the United States and Japan, including expanded implementation of the Enhanced Defense Cooperation Agreement, while maintaining some flexibility toward China. Associated Press reporting documented repeated South China Sea confrontations and the government’s strategy of publicizing incidents and seeking diplomatic and allied support.

- **Sources:** [Marcos Jr. Steers Manila toward Washington and Tokyo](https://amti.csis.org/marcos-jr-steers-manila-toward-washington-and-tokyo), Asia Maritime Transparency Initiative; [Philippines Urges Neighbors to Stand Together More Strongly Against China](https://apnews.com/article/4fdb6358b4267d45e1ad45e6d58e6304), Associated Press.
- **Period fit:** Directly concerns 2023 foreign-policy conduct.
- **Attribution:** High for presidential direction and diplomatic posture; moderate for outcomes shaped by allies, China, ASEAN, and the military.
- **Significance:** Shows a coherent shift from stated strategic goals to alliances, access arrangements, public diplomacy, and crisis management.
- **Cautions:** More military cooperation can increase deterrence while also increasing escalation exposure; outcome evidence is inherently longer-term.
- **Contrary evidence:** The administration continued to seek dialogue and did not convert maritime confrontations into open armed conflict.

### WEB-8B-08 — Official accomplishment reporting demonstrates activity but not necessarily successful execution

The *President’s Report to the People 2022–2023*, NEDA development reporting, and official agency releases document numerous activities, programs, policy launches, and administrative accomplishments. These are useful for identifying what the government claimed to have done. They are weaker evidence of completion, quality, cost-effectiveness, or durable outcomes without independent audit, implementation, or outcome data.

- **Sources:** [President’s Report to the People 2022–2023](https://pco.gov.ph/wp-content/themes/sage-8.4.1/assets/PRP_2022-2023_PBBM_072523_digitalfile_v2.pdf), PCO; [Philippine Development Report 2023](https://pdp.depdev.gov.ph/pdr-2023), NEDA; [2023 Statistical Indicators](https://psa.gov.ph/content/psa-releases-2023-statistical-indicators-philippine-development?vcode=h23q93), PSA.
- **Period fit:** 2023 reporting, with inherited and lagged indicators.
- **Attribution:** High for stated accomplishments; moderate or low for outcomes.
- **Significance:** Establishes an essential methodological limit: activity, appropriation, implementation, and outcome are distinct evidence categories.
- **Cautions:** Government performance reports have self-presentation incentives; many outcome indicators lag the period.
- **Corroboration:** World Bank, ADB, PSA, COA, and independent reporting.

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 — Clear governing program:** Supported. The 8-Point Agenda, PDP, budget, and security documents created a reasonably clear program.
- **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery:** Substantially supported. Budget allocations, laws, the MIF, investment reforms, security policy, and EDCA implementation show translation into formal machinery.
- **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system:** Partially supported. The administration moved major legislation and coordinated executive agencies, but execution data are incomplete.
- **8B.4 — Capable implementers:** Mixed. The government retained technocratic economic managers and appointed officials with relevant experience, but donor-linked and politically connected appointments complicate assessment of competence and independence.
- **8B.5 — Coordination and control:** Mixed. The government coordinated major national initiatives and foreign-policy responses, but food-price, agricultural, local-delivery, and bureaucratic bottlenecks remained.
- **8B.6 — From plans to practice:** Supported for laws, budget commitments, foreign-policy arrangements, and immediate rice intervention; incomplete for long-term infrastructure, MIF returns, learning outcomes, and digital-government results.
- **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed and preliminary. Growth continued and investment reforms advanced, but inflation, food prices, poverty, service gaps, and low productivity limited progress.
- **8B.8 — Learning and correction:** Partially supported. The rice-price ceiling was eventually lifted and policy was adjusted, but systematic use of audits and independent evaluation is not yet fully documented.
- **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance:** Supported in foreign-policy and food-price crisis management, with significant uncertainty about long-term risks and structural resolution.
- **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement:** Not determinable in full for a single year. Several institutions and laws were established, but durable outcomes and institutional resilience require later evidence.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

The program and formal actions were relatively easy to document through budgets, executive orders, laws, presidential reports, NEDA, PSA, World Bank, and official foreign-policy records. The principal difficulties concern effectiveness:

- government accomplishment reports measure activity and intent more readily than quality or results;
- several sources were inaccessible because of cache misses, 403 restrictions, or incomplete web archives;
- many 2023 outcomes reflect prior administrations, pandemic recovery, global conditions, or local-government delivery;
- growth and investment figures do not establish broad welfare gains;
- large laws such as the MIF had long implementation lags;
- foreign-policy and security outcomes are difficult to evaluate within one year;
- budget appropriations do not prove actual disbursement, procurement quality, or territorial reach.

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Executed 2023 budget and obligation/disbursement data by department and program.
- COA performance audits of infrastructure, 4Ps, rice programs, health, education, and digitalization.
- Philippine Development Plan monitoring indicators and 2023 implementation annexes.
- Actual MIF capitalization, board appointments, investment pipeline, and 2024 operational results.
- DepEd learning-recovery and classroom-delivery data.
- Food-security, rice-importation, agricultural-productivity, and price-stabilization records.
- Digital-government adoption, interoperability, cybersecurity, and service-delivery data.
- Appointment records and performance evaluations for key economic, agriculture, infrastructure, and security officials.
- Independent evaluation of the administration’s foreign-policy and EDCA strategy.

Source count: 12 candidates inspected; 8 developed evidence records; 7 accepted or context records and 5 corroborating/lead sources; several official pages were inaccessible or incomplete.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

# Review round 1 — Chapter 1B

## Status

The selected theme was 1B only: nuclear and existential-risk responsibility. The review-round search materially improved the cyber-security record and recovered the National Security Policy’s enabling instrument. Nuclear evidence remains mostly programmatic and preparatory rather than operational because the Philippines had no operating nuclear-power reactor or nuclear-weapons arsenal in 2023.

I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json`: the workspace was read-only and the shell inspection was blocked by the execution sandbox. The new records below use continuation IDs and preserve exact `methodology_ids` mappings for later manifest insertion.

## Orientation

Marcos had authority over national security, foreign affairs, energy policy, DICT, and executive agencies, but implementation was distributed across Congress, the National Security Council, DOE, DOST/PNRI, DICT, PNP, NPC, the military, and international bodies. Inherited conditions included no operating nuclear-power program, an existing peaceful nuclear-research and radiation-regulation system, substantial dependence on imported energy, and incomplete nuclear infrastructure identified through prior IAEA review.

In 2023, the administration formally adopted the National Security Policy 2023–2028 and continued developing a possible nuclear-energy program. The strongest new adverse evidence concerns cyber preparedness: law-enforcement, statistics, health-insurance, and legislative systems experienced alleged or confirmed incidents, while agencies sometimes disagreed about whether events constituted breaches. The government did respond through investigations, containment, vulnerability testing, and a DICT–PNP coordination agreement, but public evidence does not establish that systemic vulnerabilities were fully corrected.

## Evidence records

### R1-1B-01 — National Security Policy 2023–2028 was formally adopted

Executive Order No. 37 approved and adopted the National Security Policy 2023–2028. The order states that government’s prime duty is to serve and protect the people and provides the formal basis for a multi-year national-security framework.

- **Source:** [Executive Order No. 37 s. 2023](https://legacy.doe.gov.ph/laws-and-issuances/executive-order-no-37-s-2023), Department of Energy legal archive, 2023.
- **Locator:** Title and preambular text; linked full EO/PDF.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** High for formal adoption by the Marcos executive.
- **Importance:** Establishes a dated strategic framework against which security, cyber, energy, and catastrophic-risk policy can be assessed.
- **Caution:** The accessible page exposed the order title and legal basis but not the complete policy text. Adoption therefore proves formal commitment, not implementation or operational safety.
- **Contrary evidence:** The policy text and implementation records remained difficult to retrieve; no independent audit of execution was located.

### R1-1B-02 — Nuclear governance remained preparatory rather than operational

A Philippine presentation prepared for an IAEA small-modular-reactor event described continuing work on nuclear safety, security, safeguards, radiation protection, legal and regulatory infrastructure, emergency planning, human-resource development, and stakeholder involvement. It reported that the government had established a national position on nuclear power, was assessing financing, had conducted workshops on safety infrastructure, and was continuing deliberation of nuclear legislation.

- **Source:** *Technology Development and Applications of Small Modular Reactors for SMRs — Philippines*, Philippine government/IAEA platform presentation, 2023.
- **Locator:** pp. 16–18 and 21–23; lines listing safety infrastructure, legal framework, emergency issues, safeguards, and IAEA recommendations.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence, with some inherited IAEA recommendations.
- **Attribution:** Moderate to high for the government’s program status; lower for actual institutional capability.
- **Importance:** Shows that nuclear policy was focused on building safeguards and regulatory capacity before deployment.
- **Caution:** The source is a government presentation and does not independently certify readiness.
- **Contrary evidence:** The presentation itself records unfinished legal and regulatory work and prior IAEA recommendations to broaden stakeholders and strengthen safety, security, and nonproliferation frameworks.

### R1-1B-03 — The Philippines had no operating nuclear-power reactor in 2023

The nuclear material inspected describes the Philippines as developing a roadmap and strategy for a future nuclear-power program. It discusses possible technology, financing, safety, safeguards, waste management, emergency preparedness, and grid integration, rather than operating reactors or a military nuclear capability.

- **Source:** *Technology Development and Applications of Small Modular Reactors for SMRs — Philippines*, IAEA platform presentation, 2023.
- **Locator:** Summary and technology-considerations sections, pp. 21–23.
- **Period:** Inherited and target-period baseline.
- **Attribution:** Low for Marcos personally; this is a national structural condition.
- **Importance:** It limits the scope of 1B.3 and 1B.5: there is no evidence that Marcos controlled nuclear weapons or an operating nuclear arsenal in 2023.
- **Caution:** Absence of an operating reactor does not prove complete absence of radiological risk; the Philippines retains radioactive materials, research facilities, and transport/security responsibilities.
- **Contrary evidence:** PNRI and other agencies retain nuclear and radiological regulatory functions, so safety governance remains relevant despite the lack of power reactors.

### R1-1B-04 — An alleged PNP data exposure triggered investigation and competing official accounts

In April 2023, a cybersecurity researcher reported exposure of approximately 1.2 million police-officer and applicant records. The PNP stated that it was investigating possible Data Privacy Act violations and assessing protocol lapses. It also repeated DICT’s position that there had been no hacking, intrusion, or database breach. The National Privacy Commission separately ordered an onsite investigation and required the researcher to assist its inquiry.

- **Sources:** [PNP fast-tracking probe in alleged database leak](https://www.pna.gov.ph/articles/1200321), Philippine News Agency, 28 April 2023; [Statement of Privacy Commissioner John Henry Naga](https://privacy.gov.ph/statement-of-privacy-commissioner-john-henry-naga-on-the-alleged-leak-of-personal-data-among-law-enforcement-agencies/), National Privacy Commission, 20 April 2023.
- **Locators:** PNA paragraphs 63–73; NPC paragraphs 121–129.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** Moderate. The incident involved PNP systems; presidential responsibility is indirect through executive supervision and national cybersecurity policy.
- **Importance:** Tests crisis response, transparency, and institutional coordination.
- **Caution:** The event’s technical classification remained disputed; the public record does not establish the final forensic result or the number of compromised records.
- **Contrary evidence:** DICT and PNP denied a hacking incident, while NPC considered the alleged exposure sufficiently serious to order onsite investigation.

### R1-1B-05 — Government agencies coordinated containment and investigation of an alleged PSA data breach

The Philippine Statistics Authority reported that an alleged data leak involving its Community-Based Monitoring System emerged on 7 October 2023. PSA activated its Data Breach Response Team, coordinated with the National Privacy Commission, DICT’s National Computer Emergency Response Team, and the PNP Anti-Cybercrime Group, and isolated the affected system. PSA stated that PhilSys and the Civil Registration System were not affected.

- **Source:** [On the Alleged Data Breach](https://psa.gov.ph/content/alleged-data-breach), Philippine Statistics Authority, 11 October 2023.
- **Locator:** Paragraphs 336–352.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** Moderate to high for executive-agency response; lower for Marcos personally.
- **Importance:** Provides concrete evidence of incident-response machinery, containment, interagency coordination, and public communication.
- **Caution:** PSA’s statement is an official account and does not establish the ultimate source, scale, or consequences of the exposure.
- **Contrary evidence:** The need to isolate the system indicates an operational vulnerability; no independent public post-incident audit was located.

### R1-1B-06 — Cyber incidents exposed weaknesses in government systems, while officials initiated defensive measures

In October 2023, the PNP reported that a series of government cyber incidents included ransomware affecting PhilHealth, access to PSA’s Community-Based Monitoring System, and compromise of the House website. The PNP ordered enhanced cybersecurity measures, investigated whether forensic and DNA systems had been compromised, and conducted vulnerability testing across police offices.

- **Source:** [PNP chief orders boosted cybersecurity measures amid hacking incidents](https://www.pna.gov.ph/articles/1212114), Philippine News Agency, 19 October 2023.
- **Locator:** Paragraphs 65–84.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** Moderate for national executive responsibility; immediate operational responsibility rested with PNP, DICT, PhilHealth, PSA, and Congress.
- **Importance:** Stronger than a general cyber-risk indicator because it documents incidents, response orders, and vulnerability testing.
- **Caution:** The PNA is a government newswire and reports official statements; the article does not independently verify each incident or the adequacy of remediation.
- **Contrary evidence:** PNP officials stated that key forensic databases remained intact and that the recruitment portal was not affected by the earlier breach.

### R1-1B-07 — DICT and PNP created a formal coordination mechanism for cyber incidents

On 17 July 2023, DICT and PNP signed a memorandum of agreement covering intelligence sharing, joint cyber-incident response, capacity building, technical expertise, and interoperability. The PNP also reported digitalization and anti-cybercrime equipment investments.

- **Source:** [PNP, DICT ink MOA to boost anti-cybercrime drive](https://www.pna.gov.ph/articles/1205740), Philippine News Agency, 17 July 2023.
- **Locator:** Paragraphs 61–78.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** High for the institutional action; signed by DICT Secretary Ivan Uy and PNP Chief Benjamin Acorda under the Marcos administration.
- **Importance:** Evidence that the government converted cybersecurity concerns into a formal coordination and capacity-building mechanism.
- **Caution:** The MOA demonstrates institutional intent and machinery, not successful incident prevention or remediation.
- **Contrary evidence:** Incidents reported later in 2023 show that coordination did not eliminate exposure during the target year.

### R1-1B-08 — The Civil Service Commission reported no breach of its own system but acknowledged continuing cyber risk

The Civil Service Commission stated on 20 April 2023 that DICT’s NCERT had informed it that the CSC system and database had not been breached. The CSC nevertheless emphasized the need for secure infrastructure, defensive readiness, and cooperation with DICT and other agencies.

- **Source:** [CSC Statement on the Data Breach Involving Government Records](https://csc.gov.ph/slider/csc-statement-on-the-data-breach-involving-government-records), Civil Service Commission, 20 April 2023.
- **Locator:** Paragraphs 127–132.
- **Period:** Target-year evidence.
- **Attribution:** Low to moderate for Marcos; moderate for executive-agency capability.
- **Importance:** Provides favorable and contrary evidence: one sensitive agency reported no breach, but the official statement acknowledged continuing exposure and the need for infrastructure.
- **Caution:** This is an official negative finding, not an independent penetration test.
- **Contrary evidence:** The same month, PNP systems were under investigation, demonstrating uneven security across agencies.

## Lens dispositions

- **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk:** Partially supported. Adoption of NSP 2023–2028 and continued nuclear-safety planning show formal risk-reduction intent. No evidence establishes completed implementation.
- **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** No adverse nuclear threats or nuclear brinkmanship were located. Nuclear rhetoric was principally developmental and safety-oriented, but the evidence is too thin to infer strong responsibility from silence.
- **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control:** Partially documented. Safety, safeguards, radiation protection, emergency planning, and legal infrastructure were identified as priorities, but the program remained preparatory and independent safety audits were not located.
- **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections:** No material 2023 departure from nonproliferation obligations was found. The nuclear evidence supports continued attention to safeguards, but treaty-compliance and inspection records were not fully recovered.
- **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression:** No evidence that Marcos used nuclear capability to shield aggression or repression. The Philippines had no nuclear arsenal or operating nuclear-power reactor.
- **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation:** Formal safeguards and nonproliferation were included in the nuclear-planning framework. Operational export-control and proliferation-enforcement evidence remains missing.
- **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise:** The government worked with IAEA-related expertise and involved DOE, DOST/PNRI, DFA, DILG, defense, CHED, and TESDA in the proposed nuclear infrastructure. Evidence of protected dissent or independent access is insufficient.
- **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation:** Cyber incidents elicited investigations, containment, vulnerability testing, and interagency coordination. No nuclear crisis occurred. Cyber response was active but not demonstrably successful in eliminating vulnerabilities.
- **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks:** Cybersecurity is the strongest target-year evidence. Agencies investigated alleged breaches, isolated systems, and created a DICT–PNP MOA. The record does not establish comprehensive national remediation, independent audit, or accurate incident prevalence.
- **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture:** Not determinable. Formal policy and coordination mechanisms improved the institutional posture on paper, but durable nuclear safety and cybersecurity outcomes require later audits and implementation evidence.

## Information and attribution limits

- Nuclear evidence is dominated by policy presentations and preparatory documents; no operating reactor, nuclear arsenal, or target-year nuclear accident was identified.
- Cyber incident reporting is contested: “leak,” “exposure,” “breach,” and “hack” were not used consistently by agencies.
- Official denials establish the government’s position but do not independently resolve technical questions.
- Cyber incident counts are incomplete and may reflect reporting capacity rather than true exposure.
- Presidential attribution is strongest for executive orders, national policy adoption, and interagency directives; it is weaker for individual agency vulnerabilities.
- The requested local-fact reconciliation could not be completed because the local manifest and complete package were unavailable in the read-only workspace. This remains a handoff/accounting blocker, not evidence against the ruler.

## Remaining targeted gaps

- Full text and implementation matrix of NSP 2023–2028.
- IAEA safeguards-status and technical-cooperation records specific to 2023.
- PNRI inspection, licensing, emergency-preparedness, and radioactive-material security records.
- National Privacy Commission final findings on the PNP and PSA incidents.
- PhilHealth ransomware incident report and remediation audit.
- DICT NCERT incident statistics and post-incident vulnerability assessments.
- National Security Council, DICT, and agency cybersecurity budgets and procurement.
- Reconciliation of local 1B facts and dispositions in `research-ledger-manifest.json`.

### Chapter-by-chapter disposition

- 1B: Cyber coordination and incident-response evidence materially improved; nuclear readiness remains preparatory and independently under-audited.
- 2B: Third-party monitoring and the Independent Decommissioning Body were recovered; complete weapons removal and reintegration remain unverified.
- 3B: A Supreme Court red-tagging decision materially improves the legal-remedy and physical-risk record.
- 4B: COMELEC enforcement activity provides a concrete contrary/positive check against purely aggregate election claims.
- 5B: Independent price-series and compliance material were recovered; rice-policy effectiveness remains unresolved.
- 6B: COA control findings and DepEd implementation evidence improve the delivery record, but do not establish equitable outcomes.
- 7B: The Maharlika constitutional challenge and sugar-importation hearing materially improve contrary and accountability evidence; personal-integrity proof remains incomplete.
- 8B: The new records strengthen implementation and crisis-response evidence, but durable effectiveness is still not demonstrated.

The principal unresolved gaps remain: direct COA records for the Office of the President’s confidential funds, final Sugar Regulatory Administration findings, independent cyber forensics, audited program execution, and completed outcome evaluations.
